Proof.

Let
s_{0}:\Xi\to\mathcal{S}
define the function which outputs the starting state
s\in\mathcal{S}
of a trajectory
\xi\in\Xi
. We can then prove the lemma by directly lower-bounding the right-hand side of
Equation

71
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\sim\pi\times\pi}\left[{\mathbb{D_{\text{KL}}}\left({p_{R}(\cdot|\xi_{1},\xi_{2})}||{p_{\hat{R}}(\cdot|\xi_{1},\xi_{2})}\right)}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \sum_{s_{1},s_{2}\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{S}}\mu_{0}(s_{1})\cdot\mu_{0}(s_{2})\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\Xi\times\Xi\\
s_{0}(\xi_{1})=s_{1}\\
s_{0}(\xi_{2})=s_{2}\end{subarray}}p_{\pi,\tau}(\xi_{1}|s_{1})\cdot p_{\pi,\tau}(\xi_{2}|s_{2})\cdot\mathbb{D_{\text{KL}}}\left({p_{R}(\cdot|\xi_{1},\xi_{2})}||{p_{\hat{R}}(\cdot|\xi_{1},\xi_{2})}\right)

\displaystyle\begin{split}=&\ \sum_{s_{1}=s_{2}}\mu_{0}(s_{1})\cdot\mu_{0}(s_{2})\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\Xi\times\Xi\\
s_{0}(\xi_{1})=s_{1}\\
s_{0}(\xi_{2})=s_{2}\end{subarray}}p_{\pi,\tau}(\xi_{1}|s_{1})\cdot p_{\pi,\tau}(\xi_{2}|s_{2})\cdot\mathbb{D_{\text{KL}}}\left({p_{R}(\cdot|\xi_{1},\xi_{2})}||{p_{\hat{R}}(\cdot|\xi_{1},\xi_{2})}\right)\\
&\ +\sum_{s_{1}\neq s_{2}}\mu_{0}(s_{1})\cdot\mu_{0}(s_{2})\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\Xi\times\Xi\\
s_{0}(\xi_{1})=s_{1}\\
s_{0}(\xi_{2})=s_{2}\end{subarray}}p_{\pi,\tau}(\xi_{1}|s_{1})\cdot p_{\pi,\tau}(\xi_{2}|s_{2})\cdot\mathbb{D_{\text{KL}}}\left({p_{R}(\cdot|\xi_{1},\xi_{2})}||{p_{\hat{R}}(\cdot|\xi_{1},\xi_{2})}\right)\end{split}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\ \sum_{s_{1}=s_{2}}\mu_{0}(s_{1})\cdot\mu_{0}(s_{2})\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\Xi\times\Xi\\
s_{0}(\xi_{1})=s_{1}\\
s_{0}(\xi_{2})=s_{2}\end{subarray}}p_{\pi,\tau}(\xi_{1}|s_{1})\cdot p_{\pi,\tau}(\xi_{2}|s_{2})\cdot\mathbb{D_{\text{KL}}}\left({p_{R}(\cdot|\xi_{1},\xi_{2})}||{p_{\hat{R}}(\cdot|\xi_{1},\xi_{2})}\right)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\ \min_{\begin{subarray}{c}s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\\
\mu_{0}(s^{\prime})>0\end{subarray}}\mu_{0}(s^{\prime})\cdot\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\mu_{0}(s)\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\Xi\times\Xi\\
s_{0}(\xi_{1})=s\\
s_{0}(\xi_{2})=s\end{subarray}}p_{\pi,\tau}(\xi_{1}|s)\cdot p_{\pi,\tau}(\xi_{2}|s)\cdot\mathbb{D_{\text{KL}}}\left({p_{R}(\cdot|\xi_{1},\xi_{2})}||{p_{\hat{R}}(\cdot|\xi_{1},\xi_{2})}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \min_{\begin{subarray}{c}s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\\
\mu_{0}(s^{\prime})>0\end{subarray}}\mu_{0}(s^{\prime})\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\begin{subarray}{c}s_{0}\sim\mu_{0},\\
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\sim\pi(s_{0})\end{subarray}}\left[{\mathbb{D_{\text{KL}}}\left({p_{R}(\cdot|\xi_{1},\xi_{2})}||{p_{\hat{R}}(\cdot|\xi_{1},\xi_{2})}\right)}\right],

where we used the fact that the KL divergence is always positive.
∎

C.4.2
RLHF bandit formulation

RLHF, especially in the context of large language models, is usually modeled in a
contextual bandit
setting (
(
Ziegler et al., 2019
;
Stiennon et al., 2020
;
Bai et al., 2022
;
Ouyang et al., 2022
;
Rafailov et al., 2023
)
). A
contextual bandit

\langle\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},\mu_{0},R\rangle
is defined by a set of states
\mathcal{S}
, a set of actions
\mathcal{A}
, a data distribution
\mu_{0}\in\Delta(\mathcal{S})
, and a reward function
R:{\mathcal{S}{\times}\mathcal{A}}\to\mathbb{R}
. The goal is to learn a policy
\pi:\mathcal{S}\to\Delta(\mathcal{A})
which maximizes the expected return
J(\pi)=\mathbb{E}_{s\sim\mu_{0},a\sim\pi(\cdot|s)}\left[{R(s,a)}\right]
. In the context of language models,
\mathcal{S}
is usually called the set of prompts/contexts, and
\mathcal{A}
the set of responses. Just as for the MDP case, we will assume for all our contextual bandits that
\max J-\min J>0
since the reward function would otherwise be trivial.
We model the human preference distribution over the set of answers
A
using the Bradley-Terry model
(
Bradley & Terry, 1952
)
. Given a prompt
s\in S
and two answers
a_{1},a_{2}\in A
, then the probability that a human supervisor prefers answer
a_{1}
to answer
a_{2}
is modelled as:

p_{R}(a_{1}\succ a_{2}|\ s)\ =\ \frac{\exp(R(s,a_{1}))}{\exp(R(s,a_{1}))\ +\ \exp(R(s,a_{2}))},

(72)

where
R:{\mathcal{S}{\times}\mathcal{A}}\to\mathbb{R}
is assumed to be the true, underlying reward function of the human.

RLHF is usually done with the following steps:

1.

Supervised finetuning:
Train/Fine-tune a language model
\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}
using supervised training.

2.

Reward learning:
Given a data distribution over prompts
\mu\in\Delta(S)
, use
\mu
and
\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}
to sample a set of transitions
(s,a_{0},a_{1})\in{\mathcal{S}{\times}\mathcal{A}}\times\mathcal{A}
where
s\sim\mu
and
a_{0},a_{1}\sim\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(\cdot|s)
. Use this set of transitions to train a reward model
\hat{R}
which minimizes the following loss:

\mathcal{L}_{R}(\hat{R})\ =\ -\mathbb{E}_{(s,a_{0},a_{1},c)\sim\mu,\pi_{\mathrm{ref}},p_{R}}\left[{\log\bigl(\sigma(\hat{R}(s,a_{c})-\hat{R}(s,a_{1-c}))\bigr)}\right],

(73)

where
c\in\{0,1\}
and
p(c=0|s,a_{0},a_{1})=p_{R}(a_{0}\succ a_{1}|s)
.

3.

RL finetuning:
Use the trained reward model
\hat{R}
to further finetune the language model
\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}
using reinforcement learning. Make sure that the new model does not deviate too much from the original model by penalizing the KL divergence between the two models. This can be done by solving the following optimization problem for some
\lambda>0
:

\pi=\arg\max_{\pi}\mathbb{E}_{s\sim\mu,a\sim\pi(\cdot|s)}\left[{\hat{R}(s,a)}\right]-\lambda\cdot\mathbb{D_{\text{KL}}}\left({\pi(a|s)}||{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a|s)}\right)

(74)

C.4.3
Safe and unsafe data distributions for RLHF

Definition C.27

(Safe- and unsafe data distributions for RLHF)
.

For a given contextual bandit
\langle\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},\mu_{0},R\rangle
, let
\epsilon>0
,
L\in[0,1]
,
\lambda\in[0,\infty)
, and
\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}:\mathcal{S}\to\Delta(\mathcal{A})
an arbitrary reference policy. Similarly to
Definition

2.1
, we define the set of
safe data distributions

\mathrm{\mathbf{safe}}^{\mathrm{RLHF}}\big(R,\epsilon,L,\lambda,\mathbb{D_{\text{KL}}}\left({\cdot}||{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}}\right)\big)
for RLHF as all
D\in\Delta({\mathcal{S}{\times}\mathcal{A}})
such that for all reward functions
\hat{R}:{\mathcal{S}{\times}\mathcal{A}}\to\mathbb{R}
and policies
\hat{\pi}:\mathcal{S}\to\Delta(\mathcal{A})
that satisfy the following two properties:

1.

Low expected error:

\hat{R}
is similar to
R
in expected choice probabilities under
D
, i.e.:

\mathbb{E}_{(s,a_{1},a_{2})\sim D}\left[{\mathbb{D_{\text{KL}}}\left({p_{R}(\cdot|s,a_{1},a_{2})}||{p_{\hat{R}}(\cdot|s,a_{2},a_{2})}\right)}\right]\leq\epsilon\cdot\mathrm{range}\ {R}.

2.

Optimality:

\hat{\pi}
is optimal with respect to
\hat{R}
, i.e.:

\hat{\pi}\ \in\ \argmax_{\pi}J_{\hat{R}}(\pi)-\lambda\cdot\mathbb{D_{\text{KL}}}\left({\pi(a|s)}||{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a|s)}\right).

we can guarantee that
\hat{\pi}
has regret smaller than
L
, i.e.:

3.

Low regret:

\hat{\pi}
has a regret smaller than
L
with respect to
R
, i.e.,
\mathrm{Reg}^{R}\left(\hat{\pi}\right)<L
.

Similarly, we define the set of
unsafe data distributions
to be the complement of
\mathrm{\mathbf{safe}}^{\mathrm{RLHF}}\big(R,\epsilon,L,\lambda,\mathbb{D_{\text{KL}}}\left({\cdot}||{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}}\right)\big)
:

\mathrm{\mathbf{unsafe}}^{\mathrm{RLHF}}\big(R,\epsilon,L,\lambda,\mathbb{D_{\text{KL}}}\left({\cdot}||{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}}\right)\big)\ \coloneqq\ \Big\{\ D\in\Delta({\mathcal{S}{\times}\mathcal{A}})\ |\ D\notin\mathrm{\mathbf{safe}}^{\mathrm{RLHF}}\big(R,\epsilon,L,\lambda,\mathbb{D_{\text{KL}}}\left({\cdot}||{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}}\right)\big)\Big\}.

Note: Property 1 of
Definition

C.27
is commonly phrased as minimizing (with respect to
\hat{R}
) the loss
-\mathbb{E}_{(s,a_{1},a_{2})\sim D,p_{R}}\left[{\log(\sigma(\hat{R}(s,a_{1})-\hat{R}(s,a_{2})))}\right]
(which includes
p_{R}
, the probability that
a_{1}
is the preferred action over
a_{2}
, in the expectation). Our version of Property 1 is equivalent to this and can be derived from the former by adding the constant (w.r.t.
\hat{R}
) term
\mathbb{E}_{(s,a_{1},a_{2})\sim D,p_{R}}\left[{\log(\sigma(R(s,a_{1})-R(s,a_{2})))}\right]
.

C.4.4
Negative results

In the following proofs, we will define
\pi_{R,\lambda}^{\mathrm{rlhf}}
to be the optimal policy after doing RLHF on
\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}
with some reward function
R
, i.e.,:

Definition C.28

(RLHF-optimal policy)
.

For any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, reward function
R
and reference policy
\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}
, we define the policy maximizing the RLHF objective by:

\pi_{R,\lambda}^{\mathrm{rlhf}}=\arg\max_{\pi}\mathbb{E}_{s\sim\mu,a\sim\pi(\cdot|s)}\left[{R(s,a)}\right]-\lambda\cdot\mathbb{D_{\text{KL}}}\left({\pi(a|s)}||{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a|s)}\right)

(75)

\pi_{R,\lambda}^{\mathrm{rlhf}}
does have the following analytical definition (see Appendix A.1 of
(
Rafailov et al., 2023
)
for a derivation):

\pi_{R,\lambda}^{\mathrm{rlhf}}(a|s)\ \coloneqq\ \frac{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a|s)\cdot\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\cdot R(s,a)\right)}{\sum_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a^{\prime}|s)\cdot\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\cdot R(s,a^{\prime})\right)}.

(76)

Before stating the next negative result, we prove a small helper lemma which states that doing RLHF with some reward function
R
on a policy
\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}
is guaranteed to improve the policy return concerning
R
:

Lemma C.29
.

For any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, reward function
R
and reference policy
\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}
, it holds that:

J_{R}\Bigl(\pi_{R,\lambda}^{\mathrm{rlhf}}\Bigr)\ \geq\ J_{R}\Bigl(\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}\Bigr)

(77)

Proof.

Define:

\displaystyle J_{\mathrm{KL}}^{R}(\pi,\pi_{\mathrm{ref}})\ \coloneqq\ J_{R}\big(\pi\big)-\lambda\mathbb{D_{\text{KL}}}\left({\pi}||{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}}\right)

Then we have

\displaystyle J_{R}\Bigl(\pi_{R,\lambda}^{\mathrm{rlhf}}\Bigr)\ \overset{(1)}{\geq}\ J_{\mathrm{KL}}^{R}(\pi_{R,\lambda}^{\mathrm{rlhf}},\pi_{\mathrm{ref}})\ \overset{(2)}{\geq}\ J_{\mathrm{KL}}^{R}(\pi_{\mathrm{ref}},\pi_{\mathrm{ref}})\ =\ J_{R}(\pi_{\mathrm{ref}})

where
(1)
follows from the non-negativity of the KL divergence and
(2)
follows from the fact that
\pi_{R,\lambda}^{\mathrm{rlhf}}
maximizes
J_{\mathrm{KL}}^{R}(\pi,\pi_{\mathrm{ref}})
(see
Equation

75
).
∎

We begin by proving a helper lemma that we are going to use in subsequent proofs.

Lemma C.30
.

Let
\langle\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},\mu_{0},R\rangle
be a contextual bandit.

Given a lower regret bound
L\in[0,1)
, we define for every state
s\in\mathcal{S}
the reward threshold:

R_{L}(s)\coloneqq(1-L)\cdot\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}}R(s,a)+L\cdot\min_{a\in\mathcal{A}}R(s,a),

and define
a_{s}\in\mathcal{A}
to be an action such that
R(s,a_{s})<R_{L}(s)
.

Let
\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{A}
be an arbitrary reference policy for which it holds that for every state
s\in\mathcal{S}
we have
\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a|s)>0
.

Then, performing KL-regularized policy optimization with some regularization constant
\lambda\in[0,\infty)
, starting from
\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}
and using the reward function:

\hat{R}(s,a)\ \coloneqq\ \begin{cases}R(s,a)&\text{if }a\neq a_{s}\\
c_{s}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}&\text{if }a=a_{s}\end{cases},

(78)

results in an optimal (w.r.t. the regularized optimization objective) policy
\hat{\pi}
such that
\mathrm{Reg}^{R}\left(\hat{\pi}\right)\geq L
, whenever the constants
c_{s}
are larger than the following lower bound:

c_{s}\ \geq\ \lambda\cdot\log\left[\frac{\sum_{a\neq a_{s}}(R(s,a)-R_{L}(s))\cdot\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a|s)\cdot\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\cdot R(s,a)\right)}{(R_{L}(s)-R(s,a_{s}))\cdot\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{s}|s)}\right].

Proof.

Denote by
\pi_{\hat{R},\lambda}^{\mathrm{rlhf}}
the optimal policy for the following KL-regularized optimization problem:

\pi_{\hat{R},\lambda}^{\mathrm{rlhf}}\in\argmax_{\pi}J_{\hat{R}}(\pi)-\lambda\cdot\mathbb{D_{\text{KL}}}\left({\pi(a|s)}||{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a|s)}\right).

The closed-form solution for this optimization problem is known (see
Definition

C.28
). We prove the statement by assuming the specific definition of
\hat{R}
(see
Equation

78
), as well as that
\pi_{\hat{R},\lambda}^{\mathrm{rlhf}}
has a regret at least
L
, and then working backward to derive a necessary lower bound for the individual constants
c_{s}
.

We start by defining a small helper policy. Let
\pi_{\top}
be a deterministic optimal policy for
R
and
\pi_{\bot}
be a deterministic worst-case policy for
R
. We then define
\pi_{L}(a|s)
as a convex combination of
\pi_{\top}
and
\pi_{\bot}
:

\displaystyle\pi_{L}(a|s)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\ (1-L)\cdot\pi_{\top}(a|s)+L\cdot\pi_{\bot}(a|s)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1\quad&\text{if }R(s,a)=\min_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}R(s,a^{\prime})=\max_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}R(s,a^{\prime})\\
1-L\quad&\text{if }R(s,a)=\max_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}R(s,a^{\prime})\\
L\quad&\text{if }R(s,a)=\min_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}R(s,a^{\prime})\\
0\quad&\text{Otherwise}\end{cases}

(79)

Next, we show that the regret of
\pi_{L}
is
L
. Let
\eta_{\top}
and
\eta_{\bot}
be the corresponding occupancy measures of
\pi_{\top}
and
\pi_{\bot}
. Then, we have:

J_{R}(\pi_{L})\ =\ (1-L)\cdot R^{T}\cdot\eta_{\top}+L\cdot R^{T}\cdot\eta_{\bot},

from which it directly follows that:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Reg}^{R}\left(\pi_{L}\right)\ =\ \frac{R^{T}\cdot\eta_{\top}-\big[(1-L)\cdot R^{T}\cdot\eta_{\top}+L\cdot R^{T}\cdot\eta_{\bot}\big]}{R^{T}\cdot\eta_{\top}-R^{T}\cdot\eta_{\bot}}\ =\ L.

Now, having defined
\pi_{L}
, we start the main proof. Assume that
\mathrm{Reg}^{R}\left(\pi_{\hat{R},\lambda}^{\mathrm{rlhf}}\right)\geq L
, which is equivalent to
J(\pi_{\hat{R},\lambda}^{\mathrm{rlhf}})\leq J(\pi_{L})
. By using the definition of the policy evaluation function, we get:

\displaystyle J(\pi_{\hat{R},\lambda}^{\mathrm{rlhf}})\

\displaystyle\leq\ J(\pi_{L})

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\quad R^{T}\cdot(\eta^{\pi_{\hat{R},\lambda}^{\mathrm{rlhf}}}-\eta^{\pi_{L}})\

\displaystyle\leq\ 0

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\quad\sum_{(s,a)\in{\mathcal{S}{\times}\mathcal{A}}}R(s,a)\cdot\mu_{0}(s)\cdot(\pi_{\hat{R},\lambda}^{\mathrm{rlhf}}(a|s)-\pi_{L}(a|s))\

\displaystyle\leq\ 0

We will prove the sufficient condition, that for every
s\in\mathcal{S}
, we have:

\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}R(s,a)\cdot\left(\pi_{\hat{R},\lambda}^{\mathrm{rlhf}}(a|s)-\pi_{L}(a|s)\right)\ \leq\ 0

(80)

Before continuing, note that with our definition of
\pi_{L}
(see
Equation

79
) we have:

\displaystyle\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}R(s,a)\cdot\pi_{L}(a|s)\ =\ (1-L)\cdot\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}}R(s,a)+L\cdot\min_{a\in\mathcal{A}}R(s,a)\ \eqqcolon R_{L}(s).

Now, using this fact as well as the definitions of
\pi_{L}
and
\pi_{\hat{R},\lambda}^{\mathrm{rlhf}}
(see
Definition

C.28
) we prove under which conditions
Equation

80
holds:

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}R(s,a)\cdot\left(\pi_{\hat{R},\lambda}^{\mathrm{rlhf}}(a|s)-\pi_{L}(a|s)\right)\ \leq\ 0

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\quad\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}R(s,a)\cdot\left[\frac{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a|s)\cdot\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\cdot\hat{R}(s,a)\right)}{\sum_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a^{\prime}|s)\cdot\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\cdot\hat{R}(s,a^{\prime})\right)}-\pi_{L}(a|s)\right]\ \leq\ 0

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \ \Longleftrightarrow\quad\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}R(s,a)\cdot&\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a|s)\cdot\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\cdot\hat{R}(s,a)\right)\\
&\leq\ \left[\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}R(s,a)\cdot\pi_{L}(a|s)\right]\cdot\sum_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a^{\prime}|s)\cdot\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\cdot\hat{R}(s,a^{\prime})\right)\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\quad\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}(R(s,a)-R_{L}(s))\cdot\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a|s)\cdot\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\cdot\hat{R}(s,a)\right)\ \leq\ 0

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \ \Longleftrightarrow\quad\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\in\mathcal{A}\\
R(s,a)>R_{L}(s)\end{subarray}}(R(s,a)&-R_{L}(s))\cdot\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a|s)\cdot\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\cdot\hat{R}(s,a)\right)\\
&\leq\ \sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\in\mathcal{A}\\
R(s,a)<R_{L}(s)\end{subarray}}(R_{L}(s)-R(s,a))\cdot\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a|s)\cdot\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\cdot\hat{R}(s,a)\right)\end{aligned}

Now, according to the assumptions of the lemma, we know that there exists some action
a_{s}
for which
R(s,a_{s})<R_{L}(s)
and
\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{s}|s)>0
. According to our definition of
\hat{R}
(see
Equation

78
), we have
\hat{R}(s,a_{s})=c_{s}
and
\hat{R}(s,a)=R(s,a)
for all other actions. We can use this definition to get a lower bound for
c_{s}
:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \quad\quad\ \sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\in\mathcal{A}\\
R(s,a)>R_{L}(s)\end{subarray}}(R(s,a)&-R_{L}(s))\cdot\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a|s)\cdot\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\cdot\hat{R}(s,a)\right)\\
&\leq\ \sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\in\mathcal{A}\\
R(s,a)<R_{L}(s)\end{subarray}}(R_{L}(s)-R(s,a))\cdot\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a|s)\cdot\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\cdot\hat{R}(s,a)\right)\end{aligned}

(81)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \Longleftrightarrow\quad\sum_{a\neq a_{s}}(R(s,a)-R_{L}(s))&\cdot\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a|s)\cdot\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\cdot R(s,a)\right)\\
&\leq\ (R_{L}(s)-R(s,a_{s}))\cdot\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{s}|s)\cdot\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\cdot\hat{R}(s,a_{s})\right)\end{aligned}

(82)

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\quad\lambda\cdot\log\left[\frac{\sum_{a\neq a_{s}}(R(s,a)-R_{L}(s))\cdot\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a|s)\cdot\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\cdot R(s,a)\right)}{(R_{L}(s)-R(s,a_{s}))\cdot\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{s}|s)}\right]\ \leq\ \hat{R}(s,a_{s}).

(83)

∎

We can now use this lemma to prove a more general result:

Proposition C.31
.

Let
\langle\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},\mu_{0},R\rangle
be a contextual bandit.

Given a lower regret bound
L\in[0,1)
, we define for every state
s\in\mathcal{S}
the reward threshold:

R_{L}(s)\coloneqq(1-L)\cdot\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}}R(s,a)+L\cdot\min_{a\in\mathcal{A}}R(s,a),

Lastly,
\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{A}
be an arbitrary reference policy for which it holds that for every state
s\in\mathcal{S}
,
\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a|s)>0
and there exists at least one action
a_{s}\in\mathcal{A}
such that:

a)

\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{s}|s)
is small enough, that the following inequality holds:

\log\left[\sum_{a\neq a_{s}}\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a|s)\cdot\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\cdot(R(s,a)-R(s,a_{s}))\right)\cdot\frac{R(s,a)-R_{L}(s)}{R_{L}(s)-R(s,a_{s})}\right]\ \leq\frac{\epsilon\cdot\mathrm{range}\ {R}}{2\cdot\lambda\cdot\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{s}|s)}+\log\left(\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{s}|s)\right)

(84)

b)

R(s,a_{s})<R_{L}(s)

for some
\epsilon>0
and
\lambda\in[0,\infty)
. Let
D_{\mu}^{\mathrm{ref}}(s,a)\coloneqq\mu\cdot\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a|s)
be a data distribution based on the reference policy and some
\mu\in\Delta(\mathcal{S})
. Then
D_{\mu}^{\mathrm{ref}}\in\mathrm{\mathbf{unsafe}}^{\mathrm{RLHF}}\big(R,\epsilon,L,\lambda,\mathbb{D_{\text{KL}}}\left({\cdot}||{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}}\right)\big)

Proof.

According to the definition of a safe data distribution for RLHF (see
Definition

C.27
),
D_{\mu}^{\mathrm{ref}}\in\mathrm{\mathbf{unsafe}}^{\mathrm{RLHF}}\big(R,\epsilon,L,\lambda,\mathbb{D_{\text{KL}}}\left({\cdot}||{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}}\right)\big)
if there exists a reward function
\hat{R}:{\mathcal{S}{\times}\mathcal{A}}\to\mathbb{R}
, and a policy
\hat{\pi}:S\to\Delta(\mathcal{A})
such that:

1.

\mathbb{E}_{s,a_{1},a_{2}\sim\mu,\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}}\left[{\mathbb{D_{\text{KL}}}\left({p_{R}(\cdot|s,a_{1},a_{2})}||{p_{\hat{R}}(\cdot|s,a_{1},a_{2})}\right)}\right]\ \leq\ \epsilon\cdot\mathrm{range}\ {R}

2.

\hat{\pi}\in\argmax_{\pi}J_{\hat{R}}(\pi)-\lambda\cdot\mathbb{D_{\text{KL}}}\left({\pi(a|s)}||{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a|s)}\right)

3.

\ \mathrm{Reg}^{R}\left(\hat{\pi}\right)\geq L
,

We will prove the lemma by construction. Namely, given the assumptions
a)
and
b)
of
Proposition

C.31
, we choose:

\hat{R}(s,a)\ \coloneqq\ \begin{cases}R(s,a)&\text{if }a\neq a_{s}\\
c_{s}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}&\text{if }a=a_{s}\end{cases}

(85)

where the different
c_{s}
are some positive constants defined as follows:

\hat{R}(s,a_{s})=c_{s}\ \geq\ l_{s}\coloneqq\max\left(R(s,a_{s}),\ \lambda\cdot\log\left[\frac{\sum_{a\neq a_{s}}(R(s,a)-R_{L}(s))\cdot\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a|s)\cdot\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\cdot R(s,a)\right)}{(R_{L}(s)-R(s,a_{s}))\cdot\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{s}|s)}\right]\right).

(86)

Furthermore, the closed-form of the optimal policy
\hat{\pi}
of the KL-regularized optimization problem is known to be
\pi_{\hat{R},\lambda}^{\mathrm{rlhf}}
(see
Definition

C.28
). We now claim that this choice of
\hat{R}
and
\hat{\pi}
fulfills properties (1) and (3) of the above list (property (2) is true by assumption).

Property (3) is true because every reference policy
\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}
and corresponding reward function
R
that fulfills the conditions of this proposition also fulfills the conditions of

Lemma

C.30
. Hence, we can directly apply
Lemma

C.30
and get the guarantee that
\mathrm{Reg}^{R}\left(\hat{\pi}\right)\geq L
.

All that remains to be shown, is that condition (1) can be satisfied by using the definition of
\hat{R}
and the lower bounds in Equation
Equation

86
. First, note that we can reformulate the expected error definition in condition (1) as follows:

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{E}_{s,a_{1},a_{2}\sim\mu,\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}}\left[{\mathbb{D_{\text{KL}}}\left({p_{R}(\cdot|s,a_{1},a_{2})}||{p_{\hat{R}}(\cdot|s,a_{1},a_{2})}\right)}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\mu(s)\cdot\sum_{a_{1},a_{2}\in\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{A}}\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{1}|s)\cdot\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{2}|s)\cdot\sum_{i,j\in\{1,2\}}\sigma(R(s,a_{i})-R(s,a_{j}))\cdot\log\left(\frac{\sigma(R(s,a_{i})-R(s,a_{j}))}{\sigma(\hat{R}(s,a_{i})-\hat{R}(s,a_{j}))}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ 2\cdot\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\mu(s)\cdot\sum_{a_{1},a_{2}\in\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{A}}\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{1}|s)\cdot\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{2}|s)\cdot\underbrace{\sigma(R(s,a_{1})-R(s,a_{2}))\cdot\log\left(\frac{\sigma(R(s,a_{1})-R(s,a_{2}))}{\sigma(\hat{R}(s,a_{1})-\hat{R}(s,a_{2}))}\right)}_{\eqqcolon\mathcal{IS}(a_{1},a_{2})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ 2\cdot\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\mu(s)\cdot\sum_{a_{1},a_{2}\in\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{A}}\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{1}|s)\cdot\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{2}|s)\cdot\mathcal{IS}(a_{1},a_{2}).

Next, note that for every tuple
(a_{1},a_{2})\in\mathcal{A}
, the sum
\mathcal{IS}(a_{1},a_{2})+\mathcal{IS}(a_{2},a_{1})
can be reformulated as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{IS}(a_{1},a_{2})\ +\ \mathcal{IS}(a_{2},a_{1})

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} =\ \sigma(R(s,a_{1})-R(s,a_{2}))&\cdot\log\left(\frac{\sigma(R(s,a_{1})-R(s,a_{2}))}{\sigma(\hat{R}(s,a_{1})-\hat{R}(s,a_{2}))}\right)\\
&+\sigma(R(s,a_{2})-R(s,a_{1}))\cdot\log\left(\frac{\sigma(R(s,a_{2})-R(s,a_{1}))}{\sigma(\hat{R}(s,a_{2})-\hat{R}(s,a_{1}))}\right)\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} =\ \sigma(R(s,a_{1})-R(s,a_{2}))&\cdot\log\left(\frac{\sigma(R(s,a_{1})-R(s,a_{2}))}{\sigma(\hat{R}(s,a_{1})-\hat{R}(s,a_{2}))}\right)\\
&+\biggl(1-\sigma(R(s,a_{1})-R(s,a_{2}))\biggr)\cdot\log\left(\frac{\sigma(R(s,a_{2})-R(s,a_{1}))}{\sigma(\hat{R}(s,a_{2})-\hat{R}(s,a_{1}))}\right)\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} =\ \sigma(R(s,a_{1})-R(s,a_{2}))\ &\cdot\ \underbrace{\left[\log\left(\frac{\sigma(R(s,a_{1})-R(s,a_{2}))}{\sigma(\hat{R}(s,a_{1})-\hat{R}(s,a_{2}))}\right)\ -\ \log\left(\frac{\sigma(R(s,a_{2})-R(s,a_{1}))}{\sigma(\hat{R}(s,a_{2})-\hat{R}(s,a_{1}))}\right)\right]}_{(A)}\\
&+\underbrace{\log\left(\frac{\sigma(R(s,a_{2})-R(s,a_{1}))}{\sigma(\hat{R}(s,a_{2})-\hat{R}(s,a_{1}))}\right)}_{(B)}.\end{aligned}

The term (A) can now be simplified as follows:

\displaystyle\ \log\left(\frac{\sigma(R(s,a_{1})-R(s,a_{2}))}{\sigma(\hat{R}(s,a_{1})-\hat{R}(s,a_{2}))}\right)\ -\ \log\left(\frac{\sigma(R(s,a_{2})-R(s,a_{1}))}{\sigma(\hat{R}(s,a_{2})-\hat{R}(s,a_{1}))}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \log\left(\frac{\sigma(R(s,a_{1})-R(s,a_{2}))}{1-\sigma(R(s,a_{1})-R(s,a_{2}))}\right)\ +\ \log\left(\frac{1-\sigma(\hat{R}(s,a_{1})-\hat{R}(s,a_{2}))}{\sigma(\hat{R}(s,a_{1})-\hat{R}(s,a_{2}))}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ [R(s,a_{1})-R(s,a_{2})]-[\hat{R}(s,a_{1})-\hat{R}(s,a_{2})],

where we used the definition of the inverse of the logistic function. Similarly, the term (B) can be simplified as follows:

\displaystyle\log\left(\frac{\sigma(R(s,a_{2})-R(s,a_{1}))}{\sigma(\hat{R}(s,a_{2})-\hat{R}(s,a_{1}))}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \log\left(\frac{\exp(R(s,a_{2})-R(s,a_{1}))}{1+\exp(R(s,a_{2})-R(s,a_{1})}\cdot\frac{1+\exp(\hat{R}(s,a_{2})-\hat{R}(s,a_{1})}{\exp(\hat{R}(s,a_{2})-\hat{R}(s,a_{1})}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ [R(s,a_{2})-R(s,a_{1})]-[\hat{R}(s,a_{2})-\hat{R}(s,a_{1})]+\log\left(\frac{1+\exp(\hat{R}(s,a_{2})-\hat{R}(s,a_{1}))}{1+\exp(R(s,a_{2})-R(s,a_{1}))}\right)

These expressions, together with the fact that
\mathcal{IS}(a,a)=0
for all
a\in\mathcal{A}
, allow us to choose an arbitrary ordering
\prec
on the set of actions
\mathcal{A}
, and then re-express the sum:

\sum_{a_{1},a_{2}\in\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{A}}\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{1}|s)\cdot\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{2}|s)\cdot\mathcal{IS}(a_{1},a_{2})\ =\ \sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{1},a_{2}\in\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{A}\\
a_{1}\prec a_{2}\end{subarray}}\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{1}|s)\cdot\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{2}|s)\cdot\bigl(\mathcal{IS}(a_{1},a_{2})+\mathcal{IS}(a_{2},a_{1})\bigr).

(87)

Summarizing all the equations above, we get:

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{E}_{s,a_{1},a_{2}\sim\mu,\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}}\left[{\mathbb{D_{\text{KL}}}\left({p_{R}(\cdot|s,a_{1},a_{2})}||{p_{\hat{R}}(\cdot|s,a_{1},a_{2})}\right)}\right]

\displaystyle=\ 2\cdot\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\mu(s)\cdot\sum_{a_{1},a_{2}\in\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{A}}\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{1}|s)\cdot\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{2}|s)\cdot\mathcal{IS}(a_{1},a_{2})

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} =\ 2\cdot\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\mu(s)\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{1},a_{2}\in\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{A}\\
a_{1}\prec a_{2}\end{subarray}}&\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{1}|s)\cdot\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{2}|s)\cdot\Biggl[\biggl([R(s,a_{1})-R(s,a_{2})]-[\hat{R}(s,a_{1})-\hat{R}(s,a_{2})]\biggr)\\
\cdot&\ \biggl(\sigma(R(s,a_{1})-R(s,a_{2}))\ -1\biggr)+\log\left(\frac{1+\exp(\hat{R}(s,a_{2})-\hat{R}(s,a_{1}))}{1+\exp(R(s,a_{2})-R(s,a_{1}))}\right)\Biggr].\end{aligned}

(88)

Now, by using our particular definition of
\hat{R}
(see
Equation

85
), we notice that whenever both
a_{1}\neq a_{s}
, and
a_{2}\neq a_{s}
, the inner summand of
Equation

88
is zero. What remains of
Equation

88
can be restated as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} =\ 2\cdot\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\mu(s)\cdot\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{s}|s)\cdot\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a|s)\cdot\Biggl[\bigl(&R(s,a_{s})-c_{s}\bigr)\ \cdot\ \biggl(\sigma(R(s,a_{s})-R(s,a))\ -1\biggr)\\
+&\ \log\left(\frac{1+\exp(R(s,a)-c_{s})}{1+\exp(R(s,a)-R(s,a_{s}))}\right)\Biggr]\end{aligned}

(89)

To prove property (1), we must show that
Equation

89
is smaller or equal to
\epsilon\cdot\mathrm{range}\ {R}
. We do this in two steps. First, note that for all states
s
it holds that
c_{s}\geq R(s,a_{s})
(this is obvious from the definition of
c_{s}
, see
Equation

86
). This allows us to simplify
Equation

89
by dropping the logarithm term.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{s,a_{1},a_{2}\sim\mu,\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}}\left[{\mathbb{D_{\text{KL}}}\left({p_{R}(\cdot|s,a_{1},a_{2})}||{p_{\hat{R}}(\cdot|s,a_{1},a_{2})}\right)}\right]

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} =\ 2\cdot\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\mu(s)\cdot\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{s}|s)\cdot\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a|s)\cdot\Biggl[\bigl(&R(s,a_{s})-c_{s}\bigr)\ \cdot\ \biggl(\sigma(R(s,a_{s})-R(s,a))\ -1\biggr)\\
+&\ \log\left(\frac{1+\exp(R(s,a)-c_{s})}{1+\exp(R(s,a)-R(s,a_{s}))}\right)\Biggr]\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} =\ 2\cdot\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\mu(s)&\cdot\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{s}|s)\cdot\bigl(c_{s}-R(s,a_{s})\bigr)\ \cdot\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a|s)\cdot\biggl(1-\sigma(R(s,a_{s})-R(s,a))\biggr)\\
+&\ 2\cdot\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\mu(s)\cdot\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{s}|s)\cdot\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a|s)\cdot\log\left(\frac{1+\exp(R(s,a)-c_{s})}{1+\exp(R(s,a)-R(s,a_{s}))}\right).\end{aligned}

(90)

Now, we choose to define
c_{s}\coloneqq l_{s}+\delta_{s}
, where
l_{s}
is defined in
Equation

86
and
\delta_{s}\geq 0
such that:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} 2\cdot\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\mu(s)&\cdot\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{s}|s)\cdot\bigl(l_{s}+\delta_{s}-R(s,a_{s})\bigr)\ \cdot\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a|s)\cdot\underbrace{\biggl(1-\sigma(R(s,a_{s})-R(s,a))\biggr)}_{<1}\\
+&\ 2\cdot\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\mu(s)\cdot\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{s}|s)\cdot\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a|s)\cdot\underbrace{\log\left(\frac{1+\exp(R(s,a)-l_{s}-\delta_{s})}{1+\exp(R(s,a)-R(s,a_{s}))}\right)}_{\leq 0\ (\text{because }c_{s}\coloneqq l_{s}+\delta_{s}\geq R(s,a_{s}))}\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\leq\ \ 2\cdot\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\mu(s)\cdot\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{s}|s)\cdot\bigl(l_{s}-R(s,a_{s})\bigr)\ \overset{!}{\leq}\ \epsilon\cdot\mathrm{range}\ {R}.

(91)

Note that the first inequality is always feasible, as we could just choose
\delta_{s}=0
for all
s\in\mathcal{S}
in which case the inequality must hold due to the last term in the first line being smaller than one and the last term in the second line being negative.
Now, to prove
Equation

91
, we prove the sufficient condition that for every state
s\in\mathcal{S}
:

\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{s}|s)\cdot(l_{s}-R(s,a_{s}))\ \overset{!}{\leq}\ \frac{\epsilon\cdot\mathrm{range}\ {R}}{2}.

(92)

In case that
l_{s}=R(s,a_{s})
, the left-hand side of
Equation

92
cancels and the inequality holds trivially. We can therefore focus on the case where
l_{s}>R(s,a_{s})
. In this case, we get:

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{s}|s)\cdot\lambda\cdot\log\left[\frac{\sum_{a\neq a_{s}}(R(s,a)-R_{L}(s))\cdot\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a|s)\cdot\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\cdot R(s,a)\right)}{(R_{L}(s)-R(s,a_{s}))\cdot\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{s}|s)\cdot\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\cdot R(s,a_{s})\right)}\right]\quad\overset{!}{\leq}\quad\frac{\epsilon\cdot\mathrm{range}\ {R}}{2}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\quad

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \log&\left[\sum_{a\neq a_{s}}\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a|s)\cdot\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\cdot(R(s,a)-R(s,a_{s}))\right)\cdot\frac{R(s,a)-R_{L}(s)}{R_{L}(s)-R(s,a_{s})}\right]\\
&\overset{!}{\leq}\quad\frac{\epsilon\cdot\mathrm{range}\ {R}}{2\cdot\lambda\cdot\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{s}|s)}+\log(\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{s}|s))\end{aligned}

which holds by assumption (a) of the lemma. Therefore, property (1) of the lemma must hold as well which concludes the proof.
∎

Theorem C.32
.

Let
\langle\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},\mu_{0},R\rangle
be a contextual bandit. Given
L\in[0,1)
, we define for every state
s\in\mathcal{S}
the reward threshold:
R_{L}(s)\coloneqq(1-L)\cdot\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}}R(s,a)+L\cdot\min_{a\in\mathcal{A}}R(s,a)
. Lastly, let
\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{A}
be an arbitrary reference policy for which it holds that for every
(s,a)\in{\mathcal{S}{\times}\mathcal{A}}
,
\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a|s)>0
, and there exists at least one action
a_{s}\in\mathcal{A}
such that
R(s,a_{s})<R_{L}(s)
and
\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{s}|s)
satisfies the following inequality:

\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{s}|s)\ \leq\ \frac{(R_{L}(s)-R(s,a_{s}))\cdot\mathrm{range}\ {R}}{L\cdot\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\cdot\mathrm{range}\ {R}\right)}\cdot\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{4\cdot\lambda^{2}}.

Let
D_{\mu}^{\mathrm{ref}}(s,a)\coloneqq\mu(s)\cdot\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a|s)
for some
\mu\in\Delta(S)
. Then

D_{\mu}^{\mathrm{ref}}\in\mathrm{\mathbf{unsafe}}^{\mathrm{RLHF}}\big(R,\epsilon,L,\lambda,\mathbb{D_{\text{KL}}}\left({\cdot}||{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}}\right)\big)

Proof.

We begin by showing that every
\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}
that fulfills the conditions of the theorem also satisfies properties
a)
and
b)
of
Proposition

C.31
.
Let
s
be an arbitrary state and
a_{s}
the corresponding action that fulfills the conditions stated in the theorem. We show that condition
a)
of
Proposition

C.31
holds via direct derivation:

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{s}|s)\quad\leq\quad\frac{R_{L}(s)-R(s,a_{s})}{L}\cdot\frac{\mathrm{range}\ {R}}{\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\cdot\mathrm{range}\ {R}\right)}\cdot\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{4\cdot\lambda^{2}}

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\quad

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathrm{range}\ {R}}}\cdot\lambda\cdot\sqrt{\frac{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{s}|s)\cdot L\cdot\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\cdot\mathrm{range}\ {R}\right)}{R_{L}(s)-R(s,a_{s})}}\quad\leq\quad\frac{\epsilon}{2}

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\quad

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{s}|s)\cdot\lambda\cdot\sqrt{\frac{L\cdot\mathrm{range}\ {R}\cdot\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\cdot\mathrm{range}\ {R}\right)}{(R_{L}(s)-R(s,a_{s}))\cdot\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{s}|s)}}\quad\leq\quad\frac{\epsilon\cdot\mathrm{range}\ {R}}{2}

We continue by lower-bounding the square-root term as follows:

\displaystyle\lambda\cdot\sqrt{\frac{L\cdot\mathrm{range}\ {R}\cdot\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\cdot\mathrm{range}\ {R}\right)}{(R_{L}(s)-R(s,a_{s}))\cdot\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{s}|s)}}

\displaystyle\geq\quad

\displaystyle\lambda\cdot\log\left[\frac{L\cdot\mathrm{range}\ {R}\cdot\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\cdot\mathrm{range}\ {R}\right)}{(R_{L}(s)-R(s,a_{s}))\cdot\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{s}|s)}\right]

\displaystyle\geq\quad

\displaystyle\lambda\cdot\log\left[\frac{L\cdot\mathrm{range}\ {R}\cdot\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\cdot\big[\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}}R(s,a)-R(s,a_{s})\big]\right)}{(R_{L}(s)-R(s,a_{s}))\cdot\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{s}|s)}\right]

\displaystyle\geq\quad

\displaystyle\lambda\cdot\log\left[\frac{(\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}}R(s,a)-R_{L}(s))\cdot\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\cdot\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}}R(s,a)\right)}{(R_{L}(s)-R(s,a_{s}))\cdot\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{s}|s)\cdot\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\cdot R(s,a_{s})\right)}\right]

\displaystyle\geq\quad

\displaystyle\lambda\cdot\log\left[\frac{\sum_{a\neq a_{s}}(R(s,a)-R_{L}(s))\cdot\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a|s)\cdot\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\cdot R(s,a)\right)}{(R_{L}(s)-R(s,a_{s}))\cdot\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{s}|s)\cdot\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\cdot R(s,a_{s})\right)}\right]

By applying this lower bound, we can finish the derviation of condition
a)
of
Proposition

C.31
:

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{s}|s)\quad\leq\quad\frac{R_{L}(s)-R(s,a_{s})}{L}\cdot\frac{\mathrm{range}\ {R}}{\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\cdot\mathrm{range}\ {R}\right)}\cdot\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{4\cdot\lambda^{2}}

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\quad

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{s}|s)\cdot\lambda\cdot\sqrt{\frac{L\cdot\mathrm{range}\ {R}\cdot\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\cdot\mathrm{range}\ {R}\right)}{(R_{L}(s)-R(s,a_{s}))\cdot\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{s}|s)\cdot}}\quad\leq\quad\frac{\epsilon\cdot\mathrm{range}\ {R}}{2}

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\quad

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{s}|s)\cdot\lambda\cdot\log\left[\frac{\sum_{a\neq a_{s}}(R(s,a)-R_{L}(s))\cdot\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a|s)\cdot\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\cdot R(s,a)\right)}{(R_{L}(s)-R(s,a_{s}))\cdot\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{s}|s)\cdot\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\cdot R(s,a_{s})\right)}\right]\quad\leq\quad\frac{\epsilon\cdot\mathrm{range}\ {R}}{2}

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\quad

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \log\Bigg[\sum_{a\neq a_{s}}\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a|s)\cdot\exp\Bigg(\frac{1}{\lambda}\cdot(R(s,a)&-R(s,a_{s}))\Bigg)\cdot\frac{R(s,a)-R_{L}(s)}{R_{L}(s)-R(s,a_{s})}\Bigg]\\
&\leq\quad\frac{\epsilon\cdot\mathrm{range}\ {R}}{2\cdot\lambda\cdot\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{s}|s)}+\log(\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{s}|s))\end{aligned}

Before moving on, note that condition
b)
of
Proposition

C.31
holds directly by assumption of this theorem. Therefore, we have shown that every
\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}
that fulfills the conditions of this theorem also fulfills the two conditions of
Proposition

C.31
. This must mean that
D_{\mu}^{\mathrm{ref}}\in\mathrm{\mathbf{unsafe}}^{\mathrm{RLHF}}\big(R,\epsilon,L,\lambda,\mathbb{D_{\text{KL}}}\left({\cdot}||{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}}\right)\big)
for arbitrary
\mu\in\Delta(S)
thereby concluding the proof.
∎

C.4.5
Another negative result with regularization

Proposition C.33
.

Let
\langle\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},\mu_{0},R\rangle
be a contextual bandit.

Given a lower regret bound
L\in[0,1)
, we define for every state
s\in\mathcal{S}
the reward threshold:

R_{L}(s)\coloneqq(1-L)\cdot\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}}R(s,a)+L\cdot\min_{a\in\mathcal{A}}R(s,a),

Lastly, let
\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{A}
be an arbitrary reference policy for which it holds that for every state
s\in\mathcal{S}
,
\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a|s)>0
and there exists at least one action
a_{s}\in\mathcal{A}
such that:

a)

\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{s}|s)
is small enough, that the following inequality holds:

\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{s}|s)\ \leq\ \frac{(R_{L}(s)-R(s,a_{s}))}{L}\cdot\frac{\mathrm{range}\ {R}}{\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\cdot\mathrm{range}\ {R}\right)}\cdot\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{\lambda^{2}}

(93)

b)

R(s,a_{s})<R_{L}(s)

Let
D^{\mathrm{ref}}(s,a)\coloneqq\mu_{0}(s)\cdot\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a\mid s)
. Then
D^{\mathrm{ref}}\in\mathrm{\mathbf{unsafe}}(R,\epsilon,L,\lambda,\omega)
.

Proof.

To prove the proposition we show that there exists some reward function
\hat{R}
, as well as a policy
\hat{\pi}
such that the following properties hold:

1.

\mathbb{E}_{(s,a)\sim D^{\mathrm{ref}}}\left[{\frac{|R(s,a)-\hat{R}(s,a)|}{\mathrm{range}\ R}}\right]\leq\epsilon
.

2.

\hat{\pi}\in\argmax_{\pi}J_{\hat{R}}(\pi)-\lambda\omega(\pi)

3.

\mathrm{Reg}^{R}\left(\hat{\pi}\right)\geq L
.

In particular, we choose:

\hat{R}(s,a)\ \coloneqq\ \begin{cases}R(s,a)&\text{if }a\neq a_{s}\\
c_{s}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}&\text{if }a=a_{s}\end{cases},

(94)

where the different
c_{s}
are some positive constants defined as follows:

\hat{R}(s,a_{s})=c_{s}\ \coloneqq\max\left(R(s,a_{s}),\ \lambda\cdot\log\left[\frac{\sum_{a\neq a_{s}}(R(s,a)-R_{L}(s))\cdot\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a|s)\cdot\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\cdot R(s,a)\right)}{(R_{L}(s)-R(s,a_{s}))\cdot\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{s}|s)}\right]\right).

(95)

Furthermore, the closed-form of the optimal policy
\hat{\pi}
of the KL-regularized optimization problem is known to be
\pi_{\hat{R},\lambda}^{\mathrm{rlhf}}
(see
Definition

C.28
). We now claim that this choice of
\hat{R}
and
\hat{\pi}
fulfills properties (1) and (3) of the lemma (property (2) is true by assumption).

Property (3) is true because every reference policy
\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}
and corresponding reward function
R
that fulfills the conditions of this proposition also fulfills the conditions of

Lemma

C.30
. Hence, we can directly apply
Lemma

C.30
and get the guarantee that
\mathrm{Reg}^{R}\left(\hat{\pi}\right)\geq L
.

All that remains to be shown, is that condition (1) can be satisfied by using the definition of
\hat{R}
and in particular, the definition of the individual
c_{s}
(see
Equation

95
). The expected error expression in condition (1) can be expanded as follows:

\mathbb{E}_{(s,a)\sim D^{\mathrm{ref}}}\left[{\frac{|R(s,a)-\hat{R}(s,a)|}{\mathrm{range}\ R}}\right]\ =\ \sum_{(s,a)\in{\mathcal{S}{\times}\mathcal{A}}}\mu_{0}(s)\cdot\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a|s)\cdot\frac{|R(s,a)-\hat{R}(s,a)|}{\mathrm{range}\ {R}}\ \overset{!}{\leq}\ \epsilon.

We show the sufficient condition that for each state
s\in\mathcal{S}
it holds:

\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a|s)\cdot\frac{|R(s,a)-\hat{R}(s,a)|}{\mathrm{range}\ {R}}\quad\overset{!}{\leq}\quad\epsilon.

By using our definition of
\hat{R}
(see
Equation

94
), this further simplifies as follows:

\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a|s)\cdot\frac{|R(s,a)-\hat{R}(s,a)|}{\mathrm{range}\ {R}}\ =\ \pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{s}|s)\cdot\frac{\hat{R}(s,a_{s})-R(s,a_{s})}{\mathrm{range}\ {R}}\ \overset{!}{\leq}\quad\epsilon.

(96)

In the last equation, we were able to drop the absolute value sign because our definition of the constants
c_{s}
(see
Equation

95
) guarantees that
\hat{R}(s,a_{s})\geq R(s,a_{s})
.

Next, note that whenever
\hat{R}(s,a_{s})=R(s,a_{s})
the left-hand side of
Equation

96
cancels out and so the inequality holds trivially. In the following, we will therefore only focus on states where
\hat{R}(s,a_{s})>R(s,a_{s})
. Note that this allows us to drop the
\max
statement in the definition of the
c_{s}
constants (see
Equation

95
).

We continue by upper-bounding the difference
\hat{R}(s,a_{s})-R(s,a_{s})
. By making use of the following identity:

R(s,a_{s})\quad=\quad\lambda\cdot\log\left[\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\cdot R(s,a_{s})\right)\right],

we can move the
R(s,a_{s})
term into the logarithm term of the
c_{s}
constants, and thereby upper-bounding the difference
\hat{R}(s,a_{s})-R(s,a_{s})
as follows:

\displaystyle\hat{R}(s,a_{s})-R(s,a_{s})

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle\lambda\cdot\log\left[\frac{\sum_{a\neq a_{s}}(R(s,a)-R_{L}(s))\cdot\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a|s)\cdot\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\cdot R(s,a)\right)}{(R_{L}(s)-R(s,a_{s}))\cdot\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{s}|s)\cdot\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\cdot R(s,a_{s})\right)}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\quad

\displaystyle\lambda\cdot\log\left[\frac{(\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}}R(s,a)-R_{L}(s))\cdot\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\cdot\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}}R(s,a)\right)}{(R_{L}(s)-R(s,a_{s}))\cdot\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{s}|s)\cdot\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\cdot R(s,a_{s})\right)}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\quad

\displaystyle\lambda\cdot\log\left[\frac{L\cdot\mathrm{range}\ {R}\cdot\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\cdot\big[\max_{a\in\mathcal{A}}R(s,a)-R(s,a_{s})\big]\right)}{(R_{L}(s)-R(s,a_{s}))\cdot\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{s}|s)}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\quad

\displaystyle\lambda\cdot\log\left[\frac{L\cdot\mathrm{range}\ {R}\cdot\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\cdot\mathrm{range}\ {R}\right)}{(R_{L}(s)-R(s,a_{s}))\cdot\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{s}|s)}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\quad

\displaystyle\lambda\cdot\sqrt{\frac{L\cdot\mathrm{range}\ {R}\cdot\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\cdot\mathrm{range}\ {R}\right)}{(R_{L}(s)-R(s,a_{s}))\cdot\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{s}|s)}}

We can now put this upper bound back into
Equation

96
and convert the inequality into an upper bound for
\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{s}|s)
as follows:

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{s}|s)\cdot\frac{\hat{R}(s,a_{s})-R(s,a_{s})}{\mathrm{range}\ {R}}

\displaystyle\leq\quad

\displaystyle\frac{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{s}|s)}{\mathrm{range}\ {R}}\cdot\lambda\cdot\sqrt{\frac{L\cdot\mathrm{range}\ {R}\cdot\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\cdot\mathrm{range}\ {R}\right)}{(R_{L}(s)-R(s,a_{s}))\cdot\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{s}|s)}}

\displaystyle=\quad

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathrm{range}\ {R}}}\cdot\lambda\cdot\sqrt{\frac{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{s}|s)\cdot L\cdot\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\cdot\mathrm{range}\ {R}\right)}{R_{L}(s)-R(s,a_{s})}}\quad\overset{!}{\leq}\quad\epsilon

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\quad

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a_{s}|s)\quad\leq\quad\frac{R_{L}(s)-R(s,a_{s})}{L}\cdot\frac{\mathrm{range}\ {R}}{\exp\left(\frac{1}{\lambda}\cdot\mathrm{range}\ {R}\right)}\cdot\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{\lambda^{2}}.

The last line in the previous derivation holds by assumption of the proposal. That was to show.
∎

C.5
A regularized negative result for general MDPs

Throughout, let
\langle\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},\tau,\mu_{0},R,\gamma\rangle
be an MDP.
Additionally, assume there to be a data distribution
D\in\Delta({\mathcal{S}{\times}\mathcal{A}})
used for learning the reward function.
We do a priori
not assume
that
D
is induced by a reference policy, but we will specialize to that case later on.

We also throughout fix
\epsilon>0,\lambda>0,L\in(0,1)
, which will represent, respectively, an approximation-error for the reward function, the regularization strength, and a lower regret bound.
Furthermore, let
\omega:\Pi\to\mathbb{R}
be any continuous regularization function of policies with
\omega(\pi)\geq 0
for all
\pi\in\Pi
.
For example, if there is a nowhere-zero reference policy
\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}
, then
\omega
could be given by
\omega(\pi)=\mathbb{D_{\text{KL}}}\left({\pi}||{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}}\right)
.
For any reward function
\hat{R}
, a policy
\hat{\pi}
exists that is optimal with respect to regularized maximization of reward:

\hat{\pi}\in\argmax_{\pi}J_{\hat{R}}(\pi)-\lambda\omega(\pi).

We will try to answer the following question:
Do there exist realistic conditions on
\omega
and
D
for which there exists
\hat{R}
together with
\hat{\pi}
such that the following properties hold?

•

\mathbb{E}_{(s,a)\sim D}\left[{\frac{|\hat{R}(s,a)-R(s,a)|}{\mathrm{range}\ R}}\right]\leq\epsilon
.

•

\mathrm{Reg}^{R}\left(\hat{\pi}\right)\geq L
.

Furthermore, we now fix
\pi_{*}
, a worst-case policy for
R
, meaning that
\mathrm{Reg}^{R}\left(\pi_{*}\right)=1
.
We assume
\pi_{*}
to be deterministic.

Lemma C.34
.

Define
C(L,R)\coloneqq\frac{(1-L)\cdot\mathrm{range}\ J_{R}}{\|R\|}
.
Then the following implication holds:

\|D^{\pi}-D^{\pi_{*}}\|\leq C(L,R)\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\mathrm{Reg}^{R}\left(\pi\right)\geq L.

Proof.

Using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, the left side of the implication implies:

\displaystyle J_{R}(\pi)-\min J_{R}

\displaystyle=J_{R}(\pi)-J_{R}(\pi_{*})

\displaystyle=\big(D^{\pi}-D^{\pi_{*}}\big)\cdot R

\displaystyle\leq\|D^{\pi}-D^{\pi_{*}}\|\cdot\|R\|

\displaystyle\leq(1-L)\cdot\mathrm{range}\ J_{R}.

By subtracting
\mathrm{range}\ J_{R}=\max J_{R}-\min J_{R}
from both sides, then multiplying by
-1
, and then dividing by
\mathrm{range}\ R
, we obtain the result.
∎

Lemma C.35
.

For any
(s,a)
, we have

\frac{D^{\pi}(s,a)}{1-\gamma}=\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{t}\sum_{s_{0},a_{0},\dots,s_{t-1},a_{t-1}}\tau(s_{0},a_{0},\dots,s_{t-1},a_{t-1},s)\cdot\pi(s_{0},a_{0},\dots,s_{t-1},a_{t-1},s,a),

where

\tau(s_{0},a_{0},\dots,s)\coloneqq\mu_{0}(s_{0})\cdot\left[\prod_{i=1}^{t-1}\tau(s_{i}\mid s_{i-1},a_{i-1})\right]\cdot\tau(s\mid s_{t-1},a_{t-1}),

which is the part in the probability of a trajectory that does not depend on the policy, and

\pi(s_{0},a_{0},\dots,s,a)\coloneqq\pi(a\mid s)\cdot\prod_{i=0}^{t-1}\pi(a_{i}\mid s_{i}).

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\frac{D^{\pi}(s,a)}{1-\gamma}=\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{t}P(s_{t}=s,a_{t}=a\mid\xi\sim\pi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{t}\sum_{s_{0},a_{0},\dots,s_{t-1},a_{t-1}}P(s_{0},a_{0},\dots,s_{t-1},a_{t-1},s,a\mid\pi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{t}\sum_{s_{0},a_{0},\dots,s_{t-1},a_{t-1}}\mu_{0}(s_{0})\pi(a_{0}\mid s_{0})\left[\prod_{i=1}^{t-1}\tau(s_{i}\mid s_{i-1},a_{i-1})\pi(a_{i}\mid s_{i})\right]\tau(s\mid s_{t-1},a_{t-1})\pi(a\mid s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{t}\sum_{s_{0},a_{0},\dots,s_{t-1},a_{t-1}}\tau(s_{0},a_{0},\dots,s_{t-1},a_{t-1},s)\cdot\pi(s_{0},a_{0},\dots,s_{t-1},a_{t-1},s,a).

∎

Lemma C.36
.

Let
1\geq\delta>0
.
Assume that
\pi(a\mid s)\geq 1-\delta
for all
(s,a)\in\mathrm{supp}\ D^{\pi_{*}}
and that
\pi_{*}
is a deterministic policy.
4
4

4

In this lemma, one does not need the assumption that
\pi_{*}
is a worst-case policy, but this case will be the only application later on.

Then for all
(s,a)\in{\mathcal{S}{\times}\mathcal{A}}
, one has

D^{\pi_{*}}(s,a)-\delta\cdot(1-\gamma)\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial\gamma}\left(\frac{\gamma}{1-\gamma}D^{\pi_{*}}(s,a)\right)\leq D^{\pi}(s,a)\leq D^{\pi_{*}}(s,a)+\frac{\delta}{1-\gamma}.

(97)

This also results in the following two inequalities:

D^{\pi}(\mathrm{supp}\ D^{\pi_{*}})\geq 1-\frac{\delta}{1-\gamma},\quad\|D^{\pi}-D^{\pi_{*}}\|\leq\sqrt{|{\mathcal{S}{\times}\mathcal{A}}|}\cdot\frac{\delta}{1-\gamma}.

(98)

Proof.

Let
(s,a)\in\mathrm{supp}\ D^{\pi_{*}}
.
We want to apply the summation formula in Lemma
C.35
, which we recommend to recall.
For simplicity, in the following we will write
s_{0},a_{0},\dots
when we implicitly mean trajectories up until
s_{t-1},a_{t-1}
.
Now, we will write “
\pi_{*}
-comp” into a sum to indicate that we only sum over states and actions that make the whole trajectory-segment
compatible
with policy
\pi_{*}
, meaning all transitions have positive probability and the actions are deterministically selected by
\pi_{*}
.
Note that if we restrict to such summands, then each consecutive pair
(s_{i},a_{i})\in\mathrm{supp}\ D^{\pi_{*}}
is in the support of
D^{\pi_{*}}
, and thus we can use our assumption
\pi(a_{i}\mid s_{i})\geq 1-\delta
on those.
We can use this strategy for a lower-bound:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{D^{\pi}(s,a)}{1-\gamma}&\geq\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{t}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s_{0},a_{0},\dots\\
\pi_{*}-\mathrm{comp}\end{subarray}}\tau(s_{0},a_{0},\dots,s)\cdot\pi(s_{0},a_{0},\dots,s,a)\\
&\geq\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{t}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s_{0},a_{0},\dots\\
\pi_{*}-\mathrm{comp}\end{subarray}}\tau(s_{0},a_{0},\dots,s)\cdot(1-\delta)^{t+1}\\
&\geq\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{t}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s_{0},a_{0},\dots\\
\pi_{*}-\mathrm{comp}\end{subarray}}\tau(s_{0},a_{0},\dots,s)\cdot\big(1-\delta\cdot(t+1)\big).\end{split}

(99)

In the last step, we used the classical formula
(1-\delta)^{t}\geq 1-\delta\cdot t
, which can easily be proved by induction over
t
.
Now, we split the sum up into two parts.
For the first part, we note:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{t}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s_{0},a_{0},\dots\\
\pi_{*}-\mathrm{comp}\end{subarray}}\tau(s_{0},a_{0},\dots,s)\cdot 1&=\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{t}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s_{0},a_{0},\dots\\
\pi_{*}-\mathrm{comp}\end{subarray}}\tau(s_{0},a_{0},\dots,s)\cdot\pi_{*}(s_{0},a_{0},\dots,s,a)\\
&=\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{t}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s_{0},a_{0},\dots\end{subarray}}\tau(s_{0},a_{0},\dots,s)\cdot\pi_{*}(s_{0},a_{0},\dots,s,a)\\
&=\frac{D^{\pi_{*}}(s,a)}{1-\gamma}.\end{split}

(100)

For the second part, we similarly compute:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}(t+1)\gamma^{t}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s_{0},a_{0},\dots\\
\pi_{*}-\text{comp}\end{subarray}}\tau(s_{0},a_{0},\dots,s)&=\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\frac{\partial}{\partial\gamma}\gamma^{t+1}P(s_{t}=s,a_{t}=a\mid\pi_{*})\\
&=\frac{\partial}{\partial\gamma}\left(\frac{\gamma}{1-\gamma}\cdot D^{\pi_{*}}(s,a)\right).\end{split}

(101)

Putting
Equations

100
and
101
into
Equation

99
gives the first equation of
Equation

97
for the case that
(s,a)\in\mathrm{supp}\ D^{\pi_{*}}
.
For the case that
(s,a)\notin\mathrm{supp}\ D^{\pi_{*}}(s,a)
, the inequality is trivial since then
D^{\pi_{*}}(s,a)=0
and since the stated derivative is easily shown to be non-negative by writing out the occupancy explicitly (i.e., by reversing the previous computation).

This then implies

\displaystyle D^{\pi}(\mathrm{supp}\ D^{\pi_{*}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{(s,a)\in\mathrm{supp}\ D^{\pi_{*}}}D^{\pi}(s,a)

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{(s,a)\in\mathrm{supp}\ D^{\pi_{*}}}\left(D^{\pi_{*}}(s,a)-\delta\cdot(1-\gamma)\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial\gamma}\left(\frac{\gamma}{1-\gamma}D^{\pi_{*}}(s,a)\right)\right)

\displaystyle=1-\delta\cdot(1-\gamma)\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial\gamma}\left(\frac{\gamma}{1-\gamma}\sum_{(s,a)\in\mathrm{supp}\ D^{\pi_{*}}}D^{\pi_{*}}(s,a)\right)

\displaystyle=1-\delta\cdot(1-\gamma)\cdot\frac{1}{(1-\gamma)^{2}}

\displaystyle=1-\frac{\delta}{1-\gamma}.

This shows the first inequality in
Equation

98
.
To show the second inequality in
Equation

97
, we use the first one and compute:

\displaystyle D^{\pi}(s,a)

\displaystyle=1-\sum_{(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})\neq(s,a)}D^{\pi}(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq 1-\sum_{(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})\in\mathrm{supp}\ D^{\pi_{*}}\setminus\{(s,a)\}}D^{\pi}(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq 1-\sum_{(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})\in\mathrm{supp}\ D^{\pi_{*}}\setminus\{(s,a)\}}D^{\pi_{*}}(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ +\sum_{(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})\in\mathrm{supp}\ D^{\pi_{*}}\setminus\{(s,a)\}}\delta\cdot(1-\gamma)\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial\gamma}\left(\frac{\gamma}{1-\gamma}D^{\pi_{*}}(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})\right)

\displaystyle\leq D^{\pi_{*}}(s,a)+\frac{\delta}{1-\gamma},

where in the last step we again used the trick of the previous computation of pulling the sum through the derivative.
Finally, we prove the second inequality in
Equation

98
, using what we know so far.
First, note that

\delta\cdot(1-\gamma)\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial\gamma}\left(\frac{\gamma}{1-\gamma}D^{\pi_{*}}(s,a)\right)\leq\frac{\delta}{1-\gamma}

since we showed that the left-hand-side is non-negative and sums to the right-hand-side over all
(s,a)
.
Consequently, we obtain:

\displaystyle\|D^{\pi}-D^{\pi_{*}}\|

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\sum_{(s,a)}\big(D^{\pi}(s,a)-D^{\pi_{*}}(s,a)\big)^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\sum_{(s,a)}\left|\frac{\delta}{1-\gamma}\right|^{2}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{|{\mathcal{S}{\times}\mathcal{A}}|}\cdot\frac{\delta}{1-\gamma}.

This finishes the proof.
∎

We now fix more constants and notation.
Define
\mathcal{S}_{0}\coloneqq\mathrm{supp}\ \mu_{0}
as the support of
\mu_{0}
, and more generally
\mathcal{S}_{t}
as the states reachable within
t
timesteps using the fixed worst-case policy
\pi_{*}
:

\mathcal{S}_{t}\coloneqq\Big\{s\ \ \big|\ \ \exists\pi_{*}-\text{compatible sequence }s_{0},a_{0},\dots,s_{k-1},a_{k-1},s\text{ for $k\leq t$}\Big\}.

Since there are only finitely many states and
\mathcal{S}_{t}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{t+1}
, there is a
t_{0}
such that
\mathcal{S}_{t_{0}}
is maximal.
Set
D^{\pi_{*}}(s)\coloneqq\sum_{a}D^{\pi_{*}}(s,a)
.
Recall the notation
\tau
from Lemma
C.35
.
Define the following constant which, given the MDP, only depends on
\delta>0
and
\pi_{*}
:

C(\delta,\pi_{*},\mu_{0},\tau,\gamma)\coloneqq\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\in[0:t_{0}]\\
s_{0},a_{0},\dots,s_{t-1},a_{t-1},s:\ \pi_{*}-\text{comp}\end{subarray}}\gamma^{t}\tau(s_{0},a_{0}\dots,s)\cdot(1-\delta)^{t}\cdot\delta>0.

(102)

We get the following result:

Lemma C.37
.

Define the reward function
\hat{R}:{\mathcal{S}{\times}\mathcal{A}}\to\mathbb{R}
as follows:

\hat{R}(s,a)\coloneqq\begin{cases}R(s,a),\ \ (s,a)\notin\mathrm{supp}\ D^{\pi_{*}},\\
\max R+\frac{\lambda}{C(\delta,\pi_{*},\mu_{0},\tau,\gamma)}\cdot\omega(\pi_{*}),\ \text{else}.\end{cases}

(103)

Assume that
\hat{\pi}
is
(\lambda,\omega)
-RLHF optimal with respect to
\hat{R}
.
Then for all
(s,a)\in\mathrm{supp}\ D^{\pi_{*}}
, we have
\hat{\pi}(a\mid s)\geq 1-\delta
.

Proof.

We show this statement by induction over the number of timesteps that
\pi_{*}
needs to reach a given state.
Thus, first assume
s\in\mathcal{S}_{0}
and
a=\pi_{*}(s)
.
We do a proof by contradiction.
Thus, assume that
\hat{\pi}(a\mid s)<1-\delta
.
This means that
\sum_{a^{\prime}\neq a}\hat{\pi}(a^{\prime}\mid s)\geq\delta
, and consequently

\sum_{a^{\prime}\neq a}D^{\hat{\pi}}(s,a^{\prime})\geq\mu_{0}(s)\cdot\delta\geq C(\delta,\pi_{*},\mu_{0},\tau,\gamma).

(104)

We now claim that from this it follows that
\pi_{*}
is more optimal than
\hat{\pi}
with respect to RLHF, a contradiction to the optimality of
\hat{\pi}
.
Indeed:

\displaystyle\begin{split}J_{\hat{R}}(\hat{\pi})-\lambda\omega(\hat{\pi})&\overset{(1)}{\leq}J_{\hat{R}}(\hat{\pi})\\
&\overset{(2)}{=}\sum_{a^{\prime}\neq a}D^{\hat{\pi}}(s,a^{\prime})\cdot R(s,a^{\prime})+\sum_{(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})\notin\{s\}\times\mathcal{A}\setminus\{a\}}D^{\hat{\pi}}(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})\cdot\hat{R}(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})\\
&\overset{(3)}{\leq}\sum_{a^{\prime}\neq a}D^{\hat{\pi}}(s,a^{\prime})\cdot\max R+\hat{R}(s,a)\cdot\sum_{(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})\notin\{s\}\times\mathcal{A}\setminus\{a\}}D^{\hat{\pi}}(s^{\prime},a^{\prime\prime})\\
&=\sum_{a^{\prime}\neq a}D^{\hat{\pi}}(s,a^{\prime})\cdot\max R+\left(1-\sum_{a^{\prime}\neq a}D^{\hat{\pi}}(s,a^{\prime})\right)\cdot\hat{R}(s,a)\\
&\overset{(4)}{\leq}C(\delta,\pi_{*},\mu_{0},\tau,\gamma)\cdot\max R+\big(1-C(\delta,\pi_{*},\mu_{0},\tau,\gamma)\big)\cdot\hat{R}(s,a)\\
&\overset{(5)}{=}J_{\hat{R}}(\pi_{*})+C(\delta,\pi_{*},\mu_{0},\tau,\gamma)\cdot\Big(\max R-\hat{R}(s,a)\Big)\\
&\overset{(6)}{=}J_{\hat{R}}(\pi_{*})-C(\delta,\pi_{*},\mu_{0},\tau,\gamma)\cdot\frac{\lambda}{C(\delta,\pi_{*},\mu_{0},\tau,\gamma)}\cdot\omega(\pi_{*})\\
&=J_{\hat{R}}(\pi_{*})-\lambda\omega(\pi_{*}).\end{split}

(105)

In step (1), we use the non-negativity of
\omega
.
In step (2), we use that
(s,a^{\prime})\notin\mathrm{supp}\ D^{\pi_{*}}
, and so
\hat{R}(s,a^{\prime})=R(s,a^{\prime})
.
In the right term in step (3), we use that
(s,a)\in\mathrm{supp}\ D^{\pi_{*}}
, and thus
\hat{R}(s,a)\geq\hat{R}(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})
, by definition of
\hat{R}
.
In step (4), we use that
\hat{R}(s,a)\geq\max R
and
Equation

104
.
Step (5) uses that
J_{\hat{R}}(\pi_{*})=\hat{R}(s,a)
, following from the fact that
\hat{R}
is constant for policy
\pi_{*}
.
Step (6) uses the concrete definition of
\hat{R}
.
Thus, we have showed a contradiction to the RLHF-optimality of
\hat{\pi}
, from which it follows that
\hat{\pi}(a\mid s)\geq 1-\delta
.

Now assume the statement is already proven for
t-1
and let
s\in\mathcal{S}_{t}\setminus\mathcal{S}_{t-1}
.
Then there exists a
\pi_{*}
-compatible sequence
s_{0},a_{0},\dots,s_{t-1},a_{t-1}
leading to
s
.
We necessarily have
s_{i}\in\mathcal{S}_{i}
for all
i=0,\dots,t-1
, and so we obtain
\hat{\pi}(a_{i}\mid s_{i})\geq 1-\delta
by the induction hypothesis.
Now, let
a\coloneqq\pi_{*}(s)
and assume we had
\hat{\pi}(a\mid s)<1-\delta
.
As before, we then have
\sum_{a^{\prime}\neq a}\hat{\pi}(a^{\prime}\mid s)\geq\delta
.
Consequently, we get

\displaystyle\sum_{a^{\prime}\neq a}D^{\hat{\pi}}(s,a^{\prime})

\displaystyle\geq\gamma^{t}\cdot\sum_{a^{\prime}\neq a}\tau(s_{0},a_{0},\dots,s)\cdot\hat{\pi}(s_{0},a_{0},\dots,s,a^{\prime})

\displaystyle\geq\gamma^{t}\cdot\tau(s_{0},a_{0},\dots,s)\cdot(1-\delta)^{t}\cdot\delta

\displaystyle\geq C(\delta,\pi_{*},\mu_{0},\tau,\gamma)

Then the same computation as in
Equation

105
leads to the same contradiction again, and we are done.
∎

Theorem C.38
.

Define

\delta\coloneqq\frac{(1-\gamma)\cdot(1-L)\cdot\mathrm{range}\ J_{R}}{\sqrt{|{\mathcal{S}{\times}\mathcal{A}}|}\cdot\|R\|}>0.

Let
\mathcal{M}=\langle\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},\tau,\mu_{0},R,\gamma\rangle
be our MDP.
Set

C\coloneqq C(\mathcal{M},\pi_{*},L,\lambda,\omega)\coloneqq\frac{\lambda\cdot\omega(\pi_{*})}{\mathrm{range}\ R\cdot C(\delta,\pi_{*},\mu_{0},\tau,\gamma)}<\infty,

(106)

with the “inner”
C(\delta,\pi_{*},\mu_{0},\tau,\gamma)
defined in Equation (
102
).
Assume that

D(\mathrm{supp}\ D^{\pi_{*}})\leq\frac{\epsilon}{1+C}.

(107)

Then
D\in\mathrm{\mathbf{unsafe}}(R,\epsilon,L,\lambda,\omega)
.

Proof.

We prove the theorem by showing that for every data distribution
D\in\Delta({\mathcal{S}{\times}\mathcal{A}})
that fulfills the conditions of
Theorem

C.38
, there exists a reward function
\hat{R}
together with a
(\lambda,\omega)
-RLHF optimal policy
\hat{\pi}
with respect to
\hat{R}
such that

•

\mathbb{E}_{(s,a)\sim D}\left[{\frac{|\hat{R}(s,a)-R(s,a)|}{\mathrm{range}\ R}}\right]\leq\epsilon
,

•

\mathrm{Reg}^{R}\left(\hat{\pi}\right)\geq L
.

Towards that goal, define
\hat{R}
as in
Equation

103
and
\hat{\pi}
as a
(\lambda,\omega)
-RLHF optimal policy for
\hat{R}
.
Then
Lemma

C.37
shows that
\hat{\pi}(s\mid a)\geq 1-\delta
for all
(s,a)\in\mathrm{supp}\ D^{\pi_{*}}
.
Consequently,
Lemma

C.36
implies that

\|D^{\hat{\pi}}-D^{\pi_{*}}\|\leq\sqrt{|{\mathcal{S}{\times}\mathcal{A}}|}\cdot\frac{\delta}{1-\gamma}=\frac{(1-L)\cdot\mathrm{range}\ J_{R}}{\|R\|}.

Consequently,
Lemma

C.34
shows that
\mathrm{Reg}^{R}\left(\hat{\pi}\right)\geq L
, and thus the second claim.
For the first claim, note that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{(s,a)\sim D}\left[{|\hat{R}(s,a)-R(s,a)|}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{(s,a)\in\mathrm{supp}\ D^{\pi_{*}}}D(s,a)\cdot\left(\max R+\frac{\lambda}{C(\delta,\pi_{*},\mu_{0},\tau,\gamma)}\omega(\pi_{*})-R(s,a)\right)

\displaystyle\leq D(\mathrm{supp}\ D^{\pi_{*}})\cdot\left(\mathrm{range}\ R+\frac{\lambda}{C(\delta,\pi_{*},\mu_{0},\tau,\gamma)}\omega(\pi_{*})\right)

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon\cdot\mathrm{range}\ R,

where the last claim follows from the assumed inequality in
D(\mathrm{supp}\ D^{\pi_{*}})
.
∎

We obtain the following corollary, which is very similar to
Proposition

C.5
.
The main difference is that the earlier result only assumed a poliy of regret
L
and not regret
1
:

Corollary C.39
.

Theorem

C.38
specializes as follows for the case
\lambda=0
:
Assume
D(\mathrm{supp}\ D^{\pi_{*}})\leq\epsilon
.
Then there exists a reward function
\hat{R}
together with an optimal policy
\hat{\pi}
that satisfies the two inequalities from the previous result.

Proof.

This directly follows from
\lambda=0
.
For completeness, we note that the definition of
\hat{R}
also simplifies, namely to

\hat{R}(s,a)=\begin{cases}R(s,a),\ (s,a)\notin\mathrm{supp}\ D^{\pi_{*}}\\
\max R,\ \text{else}.\end{cases}

∎

We now present another specialization of Theorem
C.38
.
Namely, from now on, assume that
D=D^{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}}
and
\omega(\pi)=\mathbb{D_{\text{KL}}}\left({\pi}||{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}}\right)
.
In other words, the dataset used to evaluate the reward function is sampled from the same (safe) policy used in KL-regularization.
This leads to the following condition specializing the one from Equation (
107
):

D^{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}}(\mathrm{supp}\ D^{\pi_{*}})\leq\frac{\epsilon}{1+\frac{\lambda\cdot\mathbb{D_{\text{KL}}}\left({\pi_{*}}||{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}}\right)}{\mathrm{range}\ R\cdot C(\delta,\pi_{*},\mu_{0},\tau,\gamma)}}.

(108)

\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}
now appears on both the left and right side of the equation, and so one can wonder whether it is ever possible that the inequality holds.
After all, if
D^{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}}(\mathrm{supp}\ D^{\pi_{*}})
“gets smaller”, then
\mathbb{D_{\text{KL}}}\left({\pi_{*}}||{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}}\right)
should usually get “larger”.
However, halfing each of the probabilities
D^{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}}(s,a)
for
(s,a)\in\mathrm{supp}\ D^{\pi_{*}}
leads to only an increase by the addition of
\log 2
of
\mathbb{D_{\text{KL}}}\left({\pi_{*}}||{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}}\right)
.
Thus, intuitively, we expect the inequality to hold when the left-hand-side is very small.
An issue is that the KL divergence can disproportionately blow up in size if some
individual
probabilities
D^{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}}(s,a)
for
(s,a)\in\mathrm{supp}\ D^{\pi_{*}}
are very small compared to other such probabilities.
This can be avoided by a bound in the proportional difference of these probabilities.
We thus obtain the following sufficient condition for a ‘‘negative result’’:
5
5

5

The condition is quite strong and we would welcome attempts to weaken it.

Corollary C.40
.

Let the notation be as in Theorem
C.38
and assume
D=D^{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}}
and
\omega(\pi)=\mathbb{D_{\text{KL}}}\left({\pi}||{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}}\right)
.
Let
K\geq 0
be a constant such that

\max_{(s,a)\in\mathrm{supp}\ D^{\pi_{*}}}D^{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}}(s,a)\leq K\cdot\min_{(s,a)\in\mathrm{supp}\ D^{\pi_{*}}}D^{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}}(s,a).

Assume that

\min_{(s,a)\in\mathrm{supp}\ D^{\pi_{*}}}D^{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}}(s,a)\leq\left(\frac{\epsilon}{K\cdot|\mathcal{S}|\cdot\left(1+\frac{\lambda}{\mathrm{range}\ R\cdot C(\delta,\pi_{*},\mu_{0},\tau,\gamma)}\right)}\right)^{2}.

(109)

Then Equation (
107
) holds, and the conclusion of the theorem thus follows.

Proof.

As argued before, the equation to show can be written as Equation (
108
).
We can upper-bound the left-hand-side as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{split}D^{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}}(\mathrm{supp}\ D^{\pi_{*}})&=\sum_{(s,a)\in\mathrm{supp}\ D^{\pi_{*}}}D^{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}}(s,a)\\
&\leq|\mathrm{supp}\ D^{\pi_{*}}|\cdot\max_{(s,a)\in\mathrm{supp}\ D^{\pi_{*}}}D^{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}}(s,a)\\
&\leq|\mathcal{S}|\cdot K\cdot\min_{(s,a)\in\mathrm{supp}\ D^{\pi_{*}}}D^{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}}(s,a).\end{split}

(110)

In one step, we used that
\pi_{*}
is assumed to be deterministic, which leads to a bound in the size of the support.
Now, we lower-bound the other side by noting that

\displaystyle\mathbb{D_{\text{KL}}}\left({\pi_{*}}||{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(s,a)\in\mathrm{supp}\ D^{\pi_{*}}}D^{\pi_{*}}(s,a)\cdot\log\frac{D^{\pi_{*}}(s,a)}{D^{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}}(s,a)}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{(s,a)\in\mathrm{supp}\ D^{\pi_{*}}}D^{\pi_{*}}(s,a)\cdot\log\frac{1}{\min_{(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})\in\mathrm{supp}\ D^{\pi_{*}}}D^{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}}(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=\log\frac{1}{\min_{(s,a)\in\mathrm{supp}\ D^{\pi_{*}}}D^{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}}(s,a)}.

Thus, for the right-hand-side, we obtain

\frac{\epsilon}{1+\frac{\lambda\cdot\mathbb{D_{\text{KL}}}\left({\pi_{*}}||{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}}\right)}{\mathrm{range}\ R\cdot C(\delta,\pi_{*},\mu_{0},\tau,\gamma)}}\geq\frac{\epsilon}{1+\frac{\lambda}{\mathrm{range}\ R\cdot C(\delta,\pi_{*},\mu_{0},\tau,\gamma)}\cdot\log\frac{1}{\min_{(s,a)\in\mathrm{supp}\ D^{\pi_{*}}}D^{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}}(s,a)}}

(111)

Now, set
A\coloneqq|\mathcal{S}|\cdot K
,
B\coloneqq\frac{\lambda}{\mathrm{range}\ R\cdot C(\delta,\pi_{*},\mu_{0},\tau,\gamma)}
and
x\coloneqq\min_{(s,a)\in\mathrm{supp}\ D^{\pi_{*}}}D^{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}}(s,a)
.
Then comparing with Equations (
110
) and (
111
), we are left with showing the following, which we also equivalently rewrite:

\displaystyle A\cdot x\leq\frac{\epsilon}{1+B\cdot\log\frac{1}{x}}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle A\cdot\left(x+Bx\log\frac{1}{x}\right)\leq\epsilon.

Now, together with the assumed condition on
x
from Equation (
109
), and upper-bounding the logarithm with a square-root, and
x
by
\sqrt{x}
since
x\leq 1
, we obtain:

\displaystyle A\cdot\left(x+Bx\log\frac{1}{x}\right)

\displaystyle\leq A\cdot\left(x+B\sqrt{x}\right)

\displaystyle\leq A\cdot\left((1+B)\cdot\sqrt{x}\right)

\displaystyle\leq A\cdot(1+B)\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{A\cdot(1+B)}

\displaystyle=\epsilon.

That was to show.
∎

Appendix D
Requirements for safe optimization

In this section, we answer the question under which circumstances we can guarantee a safe optimization of a given reward function. Wherever applicable, we make the same assumptions as stated in
Section

C.1
.

D.1
Applying Berge’s maximum theorem

Definition D.1

(Correspondence)
.

Let
X,Y
be two sets.
A
correspondence

C:X\rightrightarrows Y
is a function
X\to\mathcal{P}(Y)
from
X
to the power set of
Y
.

Definition D.2

(Upper Hemicontinuous, Lower Hemicontinuous, Continuous, Compact-Valued)
.

Let
C:X\rightrightarrows Y
be a correspondence where
X
and
Y
are topological spaces.
Then:

•

C
is called
upper hemicontinuous
if for every
x\in X
and every open set
V\subseteq Y
with
C(x)\subseteq V
, there exists an open set
U\subseteq X
with
x\in U
and such that for all
x^{\prime}\in U
one has
C(x^{\prime})\subseteq V
.

•

C
is called
lower hemicontinuous
if for every
x\in X
and every open set
V\subseteq Y
with
C(x)\cap V\neq\emptyset
, there exists an open set
U\subseteq X
with
x\in U
and such that for all
x^{\prime}\in U
one has
C(x^{\prime})\cap V\neq\emptyset
.

•

C
is called
continuous
if it is both upper and lower hemicontinuous.

•

C
is called
compact-valued
if
C(x)
is a compact subset of
Y
for all
x\in X
.

Theorem D.3

(Maximum Theorem,
(
Berge, 1963
)
)
.

Let
\Theta
and
X
be topological spaces,
f:\Theta\times X\to\mathbb{R}
a continuous function, and
C:\Theta\rightrightarrows X
be a continuous, compact-valued correspondence such that
C(\theta)\neq\emptyset
for all
\theta\in\Theta
.
Define the
optimal value function

f^{*}:\Theta\to\mathbb{R}
by

f^{*}(\theta)\coloneqq\max_{x\in C(\theta)}f(\theta,x)

and the
maximizer function

C^{*}:\Theta\rightrightarrows X
by

C^{*}(\theta)\coloneqq\argmax_{x\in C(\theta)}f(\theta,x)=\big\{x\in C(\theta)\mid f(\theta,x)=f^{*}(\theta)\big\}.

Then
f^{*}
is continuous and
C^{*}
is a compact-valued, upper hemicontinuous correspondence with nonempty values, i.e.
C^{*}(\theta)\neq\emptyset
for all
\theta\in\Theta
.

We now show that this theorem corresponds to our setting.
Namely, replace
X
be by
\Pi
, the set of all policies.
Every policy
\pi\in\Pi
can be viewed as a vector
\vec{\pi}=\big(\pi(a\mid s)\big)_{s\in\mathcal{S},a\in\mathcal{A}}\in\mathbb{R}^{{\mathcal{S}{\times}\mathcal{A}}}
, and so we view
\Pi
as a subset of
\mathbb{R}^{{\mathcal{S}{\times}\mathcal{A}}}
.

\Pi
inherits the standard Euclidean metric and thus topology from
\mathbb{R}^{{\mathcal{S}{\times}\mathcal{A}}}
.
Replace
\Theta
by
\mathcal{R}
, the set of all reward functions.
We can view each reward function
R\in\mathcal{R}
as a vector
\vec{R}=\big(R(s,a)\big)_{(s,a)\in{\mathcal{S}{\times}\mathcal{A}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{{\mathcal{S}{\times}\mathcal{A}}}
.
So we view
\mathcal{R}
as a subset of
\mathbb{R}^{{\mathcal{S}{\times}\mathcal{A}}}
and thus a topological space.
Replace
f
by the function
J:\mathcal{R}\times\Pi\to\mathbb{R}
given by

J(R,\pi)\coloneqq J^{R}(\pi)=\eta^{\pi}\cdot\vec{R}.

Take as the correspondence
C:\mathcal{R}\rightrightarrows\Pi
the trivial function
C(R)\coloneqq\Pi
that maps every reward function to the full set of policies.

Proposition D.4
.

These definitions satisfy the conditions of Theorem
D.3
, that is:

1.

J:\mathcal{R}\times\Pi\to\mathbb{R}
is continuous.

2.

C:\mathcal{R}\rightrightarrows\Pi
is continuous and compact-valued with non-empty values.

Proof.

Let us prove 1.
Since the scalar product is continuous, it is enough to show that
\eta:\Pi\to\mathbb{R}^{{\mathcal{S}{\times}\mathcal{A}}}
is continuous.
Let
(s,a)\in{\mathcal{S}{\times}\mathcal{A}}
be arbitrary.
Then it is enough to show that each componentfunction
\eta(s,a):\Pi\to\mathbb{R}
given by

\big[\eta(s,a)\big](\pi)\coloneqq\eta^{\pi}(s,a)

is continuous.

Now, for any
t\geq 0
, define the function
P_{t}(s,a):\Pi\to\mathbb{R}
by

\big[P_{t}(s,a)\big](\pi)\coloneqq P(s_{t}=s,a_{t}=a\mid\xi\sim\pi).

We obtain

\eta(s,a)=\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{t}P_{t}(s,a).

Furthermore, this convergence is uniform since
\big[P_{t}(s,a)\big](\pi)\leq 1
for all
\pi
and since
\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\gamma^{t}
is a convergent series.
Thus, by the uniform limit theorem, it is enough to show that each
P_{t}(s,a)
is a continuous function.

Concretely, we have

\displaystyle\big[P_{t}(s,a)\big](\pi)=\sum_{s_{0},a_{0},\dots,s_{t-1},a_{t-1}}P\big(s_{0},a_{0},\dots,s_{t-1},a_{t-1},s,a\mid\xi\sim\pi\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{s_{0},a_{0},\dots,s_{t-1},a_{t-1}}\mu_{0}(s_{0})\cdot\pi(a_{0}\mid s_{0})\cdot\Bigg[\prod_{l=1}^{t-1}\tau(s_{l}\mid s_{l-1},a_{l-1})\cdot\pi(a_{l}\mid s_{l})\Bigg]\cdot\tau(s\mid s_{t-1},a_{t-1})\cdot\pi(a\mid s).

Since
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{A}
are finite, this whole expression can be considered as a polynomial with variables given by all
\pi(a\mid s)
for all
(s,a)\in{\mathcal{S}{\times}\mathcal{A}}
and coefficients specified by
\mu_{0}
and
\tau
.
Since polynomials are continuous, this shows the result.

Let us prove
2
.
Since
\Pi\neq\emptyset
,
C
has non-empty values.
Furthermore,
\Pi
is compact because it is a finite cartesian product of compact simplices.
And finally, since
C
is constant, it is easily seen to be continuous.
That was to show.
∎

Define the optimal value function
J^{*}:\mathcal{R}\to\mathbb{R}
by

J^{*}(R)\coloneqq\max_{\pi\in\Pi}J^{R}(\pi)

and the maximizer function
\Pi^{*}:\mathcal{R}\rightrightarrows\Pi
by

\Pi^{*}(R)\coloneqq\argmax_{\pi\in\Pi}J^{R}(\pi)=\big\{\pi\in\Pi\mid J^{R}(\pi)=J^{*}(R)\big\}.

Corollary D.5
.

J^{*}
is continuous and
\Pi^{*}
is upper hemicontinuous and compact-valued with non-empty values.

Proof.

This follows from Theorem
D.3
and Proposition
D.4
.
∎

In particular, every reward function has a compact and non-empty set of optimal policies, and their value changes continuously with the reward function.
The most important part of the corollary is the upper hemicontinuity, which has the following consequence:

Corollary D.6
.

Let
R
be a fixed, non-trivial reward function, meaning that
\max J^{R}\neq\min J^{R}
.
Let
U\in(0,1]
be arbitrary.
Then there exists
\epsilon>0
such that for all
\hat{R}\in\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon}\left(R\right)
and all
\hat{\pi}\in\Pi^{*}(\hat{R})
, we have
\mathrm{Reg}^{R}\left(\hat{\pi}\right)<U
.

Proof.

The condition
\max J^{R}\neq\min J^{R}
ensures that the regret function
\mathrm{Reg}^{R}:\Pi\to[0,1]
is well-defined.
Recall its definition:

\mathrm{Reg}^{R}\left(\pi\right)=\frac{\max J^{R}-J^{R}(\pi)}{\max J^{R}-\min J^{R}}.

Since
J^{R}
is continuous by Proposition
D.4
, the regret function
\mathrm{Reg}^{R}
is continuous as well.
Consequently, the set
V\coloneqq\big(\mathrm{Reg}^{R}\big)^{-1}\big([0,U)\big)
is open in
\Pi
.

Notice that
\Pi^{*}(R)\subseteq V
(optimal policies have no regret).
Thus, by Corollary
D.5
, there exists an open set
W\subseteq\mathcal{R}
with
R\in W
such that for all
\hat{R}\in W
we have
\Pi^{*}(\hat{R})\subseteq V
.
Consequently, for all
\hat{\pi}\in\Pi^{*}(\hat{R})
, we get
\mathrm{Reg}^{R}\left(\hat{\pi}\right)<U
.
Since
W
is open, it contains a whole epsilon ball around
R
, showing the result.
∎

Now we translate the results to the distance defined by
D
, a data distribution.
Namely, let
D\in\Delta({\mathcal{S}{\times}\mathcal{A}})
a distribution that assigns a positive probability to each transition.
Then define the
D
-norm by

d^{D}(R)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}_{(s,a)\sim D}\left[{\big|R(s,a)\big|}\right].

(112)

This is indeed a norm, i.e.:
for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
and all
R,R^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}
, we have

•

d^{D}(R+R^{\prime})\leq d^{D}(R)+d^{D}(R^{\prime})
;

•

d^{D}(\alpha\cdot R)=|\alpha|\cdot d^{D}(R)
;

•

d^{D}(R)=0
if and only if
R=0
.

For the third property, one needs the assumption that
D(s,a)>0
for all
(s,a)\in{\mathcal{S}{\times}\mathcal{A}}
.

This norm then induces a metric that we denote the same way:

d^{D}(R,R^{\prime})\coloneqq d^{D}(R-R^{\prime}).

(113)

We obtain:

Corollary D.7
.

Let
\langle\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},\tau,\mu_{0},R,\gamma\rangle
be an arbitrary non-trivial MDP, meaning that
\max J^{R}\neq\min J^{R}
. Furthermore, let
L\in(0,1]
be arbitrary, and
D\in\Delta({\mathcal{S}{\times}\mathcal{A}})
a positive data distribution, i.e., a distribution
D
such that
\forall(s,a)\in{\mathcal{S}{\times}\mathcal{A}}
,
D(s,a)>0
.
Then there exists
\epsilon>0
such that
D\in\mathrm{\mathbf{safe}}(R,\epsilon,L)

Proof.

To prove the corollary, we will show that there exists
\epsilon>0
such that for all
\hat{R}\in\mathcal{R}
with

\frac{d^{D}(R,\hat{R})}{\mathrm{range}\ R}<\epsilon

and all
\hat{\pi}\in\Pi^{*}(\hat{R})
we have
\mathrm{Reg}^{R}\left(\hat{\pi}\right)<L
.
We know from Corollary
D.6
that there is
\epsilon^{\prime}>0
such that for all
\hat{R}\in\mathcal{B}_{\epsilon^{\prime}}\left(R\right)
and all
\hat{\pi}\in\Pi^{*}(\hat{R})
, we have
\mathrm{Reg}^{R}\left(\hat{\pi}\right)<L
.
Now, let
c>0
be a constant such that

c\cdot\|R^{\prime}-R^{\prime\prime}\|\leq d^{D}(R^{\prime},R^{\prime\prime})

for all
R^{\prime},R^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{R}
, where
\|\cdot\|
is the standard Euclidean norm.
This exists since all norms in
\mathbb{R}^{{\mathcal{S}{\times}\mathcal{A}}}
are equivalent, but one can also directly argue that

c\coloneqq\min_{(s,a)\in{\mathcal{S}{\times}\mathcal{A}}}D(s,a)

is a valid choice.
Then, set

\epsilon\coloneqq\epsilon^{\prime}\cdot\frac{c}{\mathrm{range}\ R}.

Then for all
\hat{R}\in\mathcal{R}
with

\frac{d^{D}(R,\hat{R})}{\mathrm{range}\ R}<\epsilon

we obtain

\displaystyle\|R-\hat{R}\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{d^{D}(R,\hat{R})}{c}

\displaystyle=\frac{d^{D}(R,R^{\prime})}{\mathrm{range}\ R}\cdot\frac{\mathrm{range}\ R}{c}

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon\cdot\frac{\mathrm{range}\ R}{c}

\displaystyle=\epsilon^{\prime}.

Thus, for all
\hat{\pi}\in\Pi^{*}(\hat{R})
, we obtain
\mathrm{Reg}^{R}\left(\hat{\pi}\right)<L
, showing the result.
∎

Remark D.8
.

If
c\coloneqq\min_{(s,a)\in{\mathcal{S}{\times}\mathcal{A}}}D(s,a)
is very small, then the proof of the preceding corollary shows that
d^{D}(R,\hat{R})
must be correspondingly smaller to guarantee a low regret of
\hat{\pi}\in\Pi^{*}(\hat{R})
.
This makes sense since a large effective distance between
R
and
\hat{R}
can “hide” in the regions where
D
is small when distance is measured via
d^{D}
.

D.2
Elementary proof of a regret bound

In this section, we provide another elementary proof of a regret bound, but without reference to Berge’s theorem.
This will also lead to a better quantification of the bound.
In an example, we will show that the bound we obtain is tight.

Define the cosine of an angle between two vectors ad hoc as usual:

\cos\Big(\ang\big(v,w\big)\Big)\coloneqq\frac{v\cdot w}{\|v\|\cdot\|w\|},

where
v\cdot w
is the dot product.

Lemma D.9
.

Let
R
,
\hat{R}
be two reward functions.
Then for any policy
\pi
, we have

J^{R}(\pi)-J^{\hat{R}}(\pi)=\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\cdot\|D^{\pi}\|\cdot\big\|R-\hat{R}\big\|\cdot\cos\Big(\ang\big(\eta^{\pi},\vec{R}-\vec{\hat{R}}\big)\Big).

Proof.

We have

J^{R}(\pi)-J^{\hat{R}}(\pi)=\eta^{\pi}\cdot\big(\vec{R}-\vec{\hat{R}}\big)=\|\eta^{\pi}\|\cdot\big\|\vec{R}-\vec{\hat{R}}\big\|\cdot\cos\Big(\ang\big(\eta^{\pi},\vec{R}-\vec{\hat{R}}\big)\Big).

The result follows from
\eta^{\pi}=\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\cdot D^{\pi}
.
∎

we will make use of another lemma:

Lemma D.10
.

Let
a,\hat{a}
, and
r
be three vectors.
Assume
a\cdot\hat{a}\geq 0
, where
\cdot
is the dot product.
Then

\cos\big(\ang(a,r)\big)-\cos\big(\ang(\hat{a},r)\big)\leq\sqrt{2}.

Proof.

None of the angles change by replacing any of the vectors with a normed version.
We can thus assume
\|a\|=\|\hat{a}\|=\|r\|=1
.
We obtain

\displaystyle\big|\cos\big(\ang(a,r)\big)-\cos\big(\ang(\hat{a},r)\big)\big|^{2}

\displaystyle=\big|a\cdot r-\hat{a}\cdot r\big|^{2}

\displaystyle=\big|(a-\hat{a})\cdot r\big|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|a-\hat{a}\|^{2}\cdot\|r\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\|a-\hat{a}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\|a\|^{2}+\|\hat{a}\|^{2}-2a\cdot\hat{a}

\displaystyle\leq 2.

In the first, fourth, and sixth step, we used that all vectors are normed.
In the third step, we used the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality.
Finally, we used that
a\cdot\hat{a}\geq 0
.
The result follows.
∎

Recall that for two vectors
v,w
, the projection of
v
onto
w
is defined by

\proj_{w}v\coloneqq\frac{v\cdot w}{\|w\|^{2}}w.

This projection is a multiple of
w
, and it minimizes the distance to
v
:

\big\|v-\proj_{w}v\big\|=\min_{\alpha\in\mathbb{R}}\big\|v-\alpha w\big\|.

We can now formulate and prove our main regret bound:

Theorem D.11
.

Let
R
be a fixed, non-trivial reward function, meaning that
\max J^{R}\neq\min J^{R}
.
Then for all
\hat{R}\in\mathcal{R}
and all
\hat{\pi}\in\Pi^{*}(\hat{R})
, we have

\mathrm{Reg}^{R}\left(\hat{\pi}\right)\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}}{(1-\gamma)\cdot(\max J^{R}-\min J^{R})}\cdot\big\|\vec{R}-\vec{\hat{R}}\big\|.

Furthermore, if
\vec{R}\cdot\vec{\hat{R}}\geq 0
, then we also obtain the following stronger bound:

\mathrm{Reg}^{R}\left(\hat{\pi}\right)\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}}{(1-\gamma)\cdot(\max J^{R}-\min J^{R})}\cdot\Big\|\vec{R}-\proj_{\vec{\hat{R}}}\vec{R}\Big\|.

Now, let
D\in\Delta({\mathcal{S}{\times}\mathcal{A}})
be a positive data distribution (positive meaning
D(s,a)>0
for all
(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}
).
Then we obtain the following consequence:

\mathrm{Reg}^{R}\left(\hat{\pi}\right)\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}}{(1-\gamma)\cdot\big(\max J^{R}-\min J^{R}\big)\cdot\min_{(s,a)\in{\mathcal{S}{\times}\mathcal{A}}}D(s,a)}\cdot d^{D}\big(R,\hat{R}).

Proof.

We start with the first claim.
First, notice that the inequality we want to show is equivalent to the following:

J^{R}(\hat{\pi})\geq\max J^{R}-\frac{\sqrt{2}}{1-\gamma}\cdot\big\|\vec{R}-\vec{\hat{R}}\big\|.

(114)

From Lemma
D.9
, we obtain

J^{R}(\hat{\pi})=J^{\hat{R}}(\hat{\pi})+\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\cdot\|D^{\hat{\pi}}\|\cdot\big\|\vec{R}-\vec{\hat{R}}\big\|\cdot\cos\Big(\ang\big(\eta^{\hat{\pi}},R-\hat{R}\big)\Big).

Now, let
\pi\in\Pi^{*}(R)
be an optimal policy for
R
.
Then also from Lemma
D.9
, we obtain

\displaystyle\max J^{R}=J^{R}(\pi)

\displaystyle=J^{\hat{R}}(\pi)+\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\cdot\|D^{\pi}\|\cdot\big\|\vec{R}-\vec{\hat{R}}\big\|\cdot\cos\Big(\ang\big(\eta^{\pi},R-\hat{R}\big)\Big)
