\displaystyle\leq J^{\hat{R}}(\hat{\pi})+\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\cdot\|D^{\pi}\|\cdot\big\|\vec{R}-\vec{\hat{R}}\big\|\cdot\cos\Big(\ang\big(\eta^{\pi},R-\hat{R}\big)\Big).

In the last step, we used that
\hat{\pi}\in\Pi^{*}(\vec{R})
and so
J^{\hat{R}}(\pi)\leq J^{\hat{R}}(\hat{\pi})
.
Combining both computations, we obtain:

J^{R}(\hat{\pi})\geq\max J^{R}-\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\cdot\big\|\vec{R}-\vec{\hat{R}}\big\|\cdot\bigg[\|D^{\pi}\|\cdot\cos\Big(\ang\big(\eta^{\pi},R-\hat{R}\big)\Big)-\|D^{\hat{\pi}}\|\cdot\cos\Big(\ang\big(\eta^{\hat{\pi}},R-\hat{R}\big)\Big)\bigg]

Since we want to show Equation (
114
), we are done if we can bound the big bracket by
\sqrt{2}
.
By the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,
\cos\Big(\ang\big(v,w\big)\Big)\in[-1,1]
for all vectors
v,w
.
Thus, if the first cosine term is negative or the second cosine term is positive, then since
\|D^{\pi}\|\leq\|D^{\pi}\|_{1}=1
, the bound by
\sqrt{2}
is trivial.
Thus, assume that the first cosine term is positive and the second is negative.
We obtain

\displaystyle\|D^{\pi}\|

\displaystyle\cdot\cos\Big(\ang\big(\eta^{\pi},R-\hat{R}\big)\Big)-\|D^{\hat{\pi}}\|\cdot\cos\Big(\ang\big(\eta^{\hat{\pi}},R-\hat{R}\big)\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\cos\Big(\ang\big(\eta^{\pi},R-\hat{R}\big)\Big)-\cos\Big(\ang\big(\eta^{\hat{\pi}},R-\hat{R}\big)\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2}

by Lemma
D.10
.
Here, we used that
\eta^{\pi}
and
\eta^{\hat{\pi}}
have only non-negative entries and thus also nonnegative dot product
\eta^{\pi}\cdot\eta^{\hat{\pi}}\geq 0
.

For the second claim, notice the following:
if
\vec{R}\cdot\vec{\hat{R}}\geq 0
, then
\proj_{\vec{\hat{R}}}\vec{R}=\alpha\cdot\vec{\hat{R}}
for some constant
\alpha\geq 0
.
Consequently, we have
\hat{\pi}\in\Pi^{*}\Big(\proj_{\vec{\hat{R}}}\vec{R}\Big)
.
The claim thus follows from the first result.

For the third claim, notice that

\displaystyle\min_{(s,a)\in{\mathcal{S}{\times}\mathcal{A}}}D(s,a)\cdot\big\|\vec{R}-\vec{\hat{R}}\big\|

\displaystyle\leq\min_{(s,a)\in{\mathcal{S}{\times}\mathcal{A}}}D(s,a)\cdot\big\|\vec{R}-\vec{\hat{R}}\big\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\min_{(s,a)\in{\mathcal{S}{\times}\mathcal{A}}}D(s,a)\cdot\sum_{(s,a)\in{\mathcal{S}{\times}\mathcal{A}}}\big|R(s,a)-\hat{R}{(s,a)}\big|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{(s,a)\in{\mathcal{S}{\times}\mathcal{A}}}D(s,a)\cdot\big|R(s,a)-\hat{R}(s,a)\big|

\displaystyle=d^{D}(R,\hat{R}).

So the first result implies the third.
∎

Corollary D.12
.

The theorem implies
Proposition

3.2
.

Proof.

Let
L\in(0,1]
and assume
\epsilon>0
satisfies

\epsilon<\frac{1-\gamma}{\sqrt{2}}\cdot\frac{\mathrm{range}\ J^{R}}{\mathrm{range}\ R}\cdot\min_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}D(s,a)\cdot L.

We want to show
D\in\safe(R,\epsilon,L)
.
For this aim, assume that
\hat{R}
and
\hat{\pi}
are given with

\mathbb{E}_{(s,a)\sim D}\left[{\frac{|\hat{R}(s,a)-R(s,a)|}{\mathrm{range}\ R}}\right]\leq\epsilon

and such that
\hat{\pi}
is optimal for
\hat{R}
, i.e. (in the notation of the appendix):
\hat{\pi}\in\Pi^{*}(\hat{R})
.
Then the last claim in
Theorem

D.11
implies

\displaystyle\mathrm{Reg}^{R}\left(\hat{\pi}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}}{(1-\gamma)\cdot\big(\max J^{R}-\min J^{R}\big)\cdot\min_{(s,a)\in{\mathcal{S}{\times}\mathcal{A}}}D(s,a)}\cdot d^{D}\big(R,\hat{R})

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{2}}{(1-\gamma)\cdot\mathrm{range}\ J^{R}\cdot\min_{(s,a)\in{\mathcal{S}{\times}\mathcal{A}}}D(s,a)}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{(s,a)\sim D}\left[{|R(s,a)-\hat{R}(s,a)|}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{2}}{1-\gamma}\cdot\frac{\mathrm{range}\ R}{\mathrm{range}\ J^{R}}\cdot\frac{1}{\min_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}D(s,a)}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{(s,a)\sim D}\left[{\frac{|R(s,a)-\hat{R}(s,a)|}{\mathrm{range}\ R}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}}{1-\gamma}\cdot\frac{\mathrm{range}\ R}{\mathrm{range}\ J^{R}}\cdot\frac{1}{\min_{(s,a)\in\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}}D(s,a)}\cdot\epsilon

\displaystyle<L.

In the first step, we used
Theorem

D.11
.
Then, we substituted the definition of
d^{D}
from
Equations

112
and
113
.
Then we expanded the term by multiplying with
\mathrm{range}\ R
in both the numerator and denominator.
Then, we used the assumption that
\hat{R}
is
\epsilon
-close to
R
in the data distribution
D
.
Finally, we used the assumed bound on
\epsilon
from
Proposition

3.2
.
Overall, this shows
\mathrm{Reg}^{R}\left(\hat{\pi}\right)<L
, and thus, since
\hat{R}
and
\hat{\pi}
were arbitrary, we obtain
D\in\safe(R,\epsilon,L)
.
This is precisely the claim from
Proposition

3.2
.
∎

We now include more discussion of
Theorem

D.11
:

Remark D.13
.

As one can easily see geometrically, but also prove directly, there is the following equality of sets for a reward function
R

\Big\{\proj_{\vec{\hat{R}}}\vec{R}\ \ \big|\ \ \hat{R}\in\mathcal{R}\Big\}=\bigg\{\frac{1}{2}\vec{R}+\frac{1}{2}\|\vec{R}\|v\ \ \big|\ \ v\in\mathbb{R}^{{\mathcal{S}{\times}\mathcal{A}}},\ \|v\|=1\bigg\}.

In other words, the projections form a sphere of radius
\frac{1}{2}\|\vec{R}\|
around the midpoint
\frac{1}{2}\vec{R}
.

We now show that the regret bound in
Theorem

D.11
is tight:

Example D.14
.

Let
U\in[0,1]
and
\gamma\in[0,1)
be arbitrary.
Then there exists an MDP
\langle\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},\tau,\mu_{0},R,\gamma\rangle
together with a reward function
\hat{R}
with
\vec{R}\cdot\vec{\hat{R}}\geq 0
and a policy
\hat{\pi}\in\Pi^{*}(\hat{R})
such that

U=\mathrm{Reg}^{R}\left(\hat{\pi}\right)=\frac{\sqrt{2}}{(1-\gamma)\cdot\big(\max J^{R}-\min J^{R}\big)}\cdot\Big\|\vec{R}-\proj_{\vec{\hat{R}}}\vec{R}\Big\|.

Furthermore, there exists a data distribution
D\in\Delta({\mathcal{S}{\times}\mathcal{A}})
such that

\mathrm{Reg}^{R}\left(\hat{\pi}\right)=\frac{1}{(1-\gamma)\cdot\big(\max J^{R}-\min J^{R}\big)\cdot\min_{(s,a)\in{\mathcal{S}{\times}\mathcal{A}}}D(s,a)}\cdot d^{D}\big(R,\hat{R}\big).

Proof.

If
U=0
then
\hat{R}=R
always works.
If
U>0
, then set
\mathcal{S}=\{\star\}
and
\mathcal{A}=\{a,b,c\}
.
This determines
\tau
and
\mu_{0}
.
Define
R(x)\coloneqq R(\star,x,\star)
for any action
x\in\mathcal{A}
.
Let
R(a)>R(b)
be arbitrary and set

R(c)\coloneqq R(a)-\frac{R(a)-R(b)}{U}\leq R(b).

Define

\hat{R}(a)\coloneqq\hat{R}(b)\coloneqq\frac{R(a)+R(b)}{2},\quad\hat{R}(c)\coloneqq R(c).

For a policy
\pi
, define
\pi(x)\coloneqq\pi(x\mid\star)
for any action
x\in\mathcal{A}
and set the policy
\hat{\pi}
by
\hat{\pi}(b)=1
.

We obtain:

\displaystyle\big\|\vec{R}-\vec{\hat{R}}\big\|

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\big(R(a)-\hat{R}(a)\big)^{2}+\big(R(b)-\hat{R}(b)\big)^{2}+\big(R(c)-\hat{R}(c)\big)^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\sqrt{\big(R(a)-R(b)\big)^{2}+\big(R(b)-R(a)\big)^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\cdot\big(R(a)-R(b)\big)

\displaystyle=U\cdot\frac{R(a)-R(c)}{\sqrt{2}}

\displaystyle=U\cdot\frac{\max R-\min R}{\sqrt{2}}

\displaystyle=U\cdot\frac{(1-\gamma)\cdot\big(\max J^{R}-\min J^{R}\big)}{\sqrt{2}}.

Furthermore, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Reg}^{R}\left(\hat{\pi}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\cdot R(a)-\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\cdot R(b)}{\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\cdot R(a)-\frac{1}{1-\gamma}\cdot R(c)}

\displaystyle=U.

This shows

U=\mathrm{Reg}^{R}\left(\hat{\pi}\right)=\frac{\sqrt{2}}{(1-\gamma)\cdot\big(\max J^{R}-\min J^{R}\big)}\cdot\big\|\vec{R}-\vec{\hat{R}}\big\|.

We are done if we can show that
\proj_{\vec{\hat{R}}}\vec{R}=\vec{\hat{R}}
.
This is equivalent to

\vec{\hat{R}}\cdot\vec{R}=\big\|\vec{\hat{R}}\big\|^{2},

which is in turn equivalent to

\vec{\hat{R}}\cdot\Big[\vec{R}-\vec{\hat{R}}\Big]=0.

This can easily be verified.

Finally, for the claim about the data distribution, simply set
D(a)=D(b)=D(c)=\frac{1}{3}
.
Then one can easily show that

\sqrt{2}\cdot\big\|\vec{R}-\vec{\hat{R}}\big\|=R(a)-R(b)=\frac{d^{D}(R,\hat{R})}{\min_{(s,a)\in{\mathcal{S}{\times}\mathcal{A}}}D(s,a)}.

That shows the result.
∎

D.3
Safe optimization via approximated choice probabilities

In this section, we will show that for any chosen upper regret bound
U
, there is an
\epsilon>0
s.t. if the choice probabilities of
\hat{R}
are
\epsilon
-close to those of
R
, the regret of an optimal policy for
\hat{R}
is bounded by
U
.

Assume a finite time horizon
T
.
Trajectories are then given by
\xi=s_{0},a_{0},s_{1},\dots,a_{T-1},s_{T}
.
Let
\Xi
be the set of all trajectories of length
T
.
Let
D\in\Delta(\Xi)
be a distribution.
Assume that the human has a true reward function
R
and makes choices in trajectory comparisons given by

P_{R}\big(1\mid\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\big)=\frac{\exp\big(G(\xi_{1})\big)}{\exp\big(G(\xi_{1})\big)+\exp\big(G(\xi_{2})\big)}.

(115)

Here, the return function
G
is given by

G(\xi)=\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\gamma^{t}R(s_{t},a_{t},s_{t+1}).

We can then define the choice distance of proxy reward
\hat{R}
to true reward
R
as

d^{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(R,\hat{R})\coloneqq\mathbb{E}_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\sim D\times D}\left[{D_{\mathrm{KL}}\Big(P_{R}\big(\cdot\mid\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\big)\ \big\|\ P_{\hat{R}}\big(\cdot\mid\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\big)\Big)}\right]

Here,
D_{\mathrm{KL}}\Big(P_{R}\big(\cdot\mid\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\big)\ \big\|\ P_{\hat{R}}\big(\cdot\mid\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\big)\Big)
is the Kullback-Leibler divergence of two binary distributions over values
{1,2}
.
Explicitly, for
P\coloneqq P_{R}\big(\cdot\mid\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\big)
and similarly
\hat{P}
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}D_{\mathrm{KL}}\big(P\ \|\ \hat{P}\big)&=P(1)\log\frac{P(1)}{\hat{P}(1)}+\big(1-P(1)\big)\log\frac{1-P(1)}{1-\hat{P}(1)}\\
&=-\Big[P(1)\log\hat{P}(1)+\big(1-P(1)\big)\log\big(1-\hat{P}(1)\big)\big]-H\big(P(1)\big).\end{split}

(116)

Here,
H(p)\coloneqq-\big[p\log p+(1-p)\log(1-p)\big]
is the binary entropy function.

Fix in this whole section the true reward function
R
with
\max J^{R}\neq\min J^{R}
in a fixed MDP.

The goal of this section is to prove the following proposition:

Proposition D.15
.

Let
U\in(0,1]
.
Then there exists an
\epsilon>0
such that for all
\hat{R}
with

d^{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(R,\hat{R})<\epsilon

and all
\hat{\pi}\in\Pi^{*}(\hat{R})
we have
\mathrm{Reg}^{R}\left(\hat{\pi}\right)<U
.

We prove this by chaining together four lemmas.
The first of the four lemmas needs its own lemma, so we end up with five lemmas overall:

Lemma D.16
.

Assume
R,\hat{R}
are two reward functions and
\pi
a policy.
Then

\big|J^{R}(\pi)-J^{\hat{R}}(\pi)\big|\leq\max_{\xi\in\Xi}\big|G(\xi)-\hat{G}(\xi)\big|.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\big|J^{R}(\pi)-J^{\hat{R}}(\pi)\big|

\displaystyle=\big|\widetilde{D}^{\pi}\cdot\big(G-\hat{G}\big)\big|

\displaystyle=\Bigg|\sum_{\xi\in\Xi}\widetilde{D}^{\pi}(\xi)\cdot\big(G(\xi)-\hat{G}(\xi)\big)\Bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\xi\in\Xi}\widetilde{D}^{\pi}(\xi)\cdot\big|G(\xi)-\hat{G}(\xi)\big|

\displaystyle\leq\max_{\xi\in\Xi}\big|G(\xi)-\hat{G}(\xi)\big|\cdot\sum_{\xi\in\Xi}\widetilde{D}^{\pi}(\xi)

\displaystyle=\max_{\xi\in\Xi}\big|G(\xi)-\hat{G}(\xi)\big|

In the last step, we used that distributions sum to one.
∎

Lemma D.17
.

Let
U\in(0,1]
.
Then there exists
\sigma(U)>0
such that for all
\hat{R}
and
\hat{\pi}\in\Pi^{*}(\hat{R})
for which there exists
c\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\max_{\xi\in\Xi}\big|\hat{G}(\xi)-G(\xi)-c\big|<\sigma(U)
, we have
\mathrm{Reg}^{R}\left(\hat{\pi}\right)<U
.

Concretely, we can set
\sigma(U)\coloneqq\frac{\max J^{R}-\min J^{R}}{2}\cdot U
.

Proof.

Set
\sigma(U)
as stated and let
\hat{R}
,
\hat{\pi}
and
c
have the stated properties.
The regret bound we want to show is equivalent to the following statement:

J^{R}(\hat{\pi})>\max J^{R}-\big(\max J^{R}-\min J^{R}\big)\cdot U=\max J^{R}-2\sigma(U).

(117)

Let
\tilde{c}
be the constant such that
\hat{G}-c
is the return function of
\hat{R}-\tilde{c}
.
Concretely, one can set
\tilde{c}=\frac{1-\gamma}{1-\gamma^{T+1}}\cdot c
.
Lemma
D.16
ensures that

J^{R}(\hat{\pi})>J^{\hat{R}-\tilde{c}}(\hat{\pi})-\sigma(U).

(118)

Now, let
\pi
be an optimal policy for
R
.
Again, Lemma
D.16
ensures

\max J^{R}=J^{R}(\pi)<J^{\hat{R}-\tilde{c}}(\pi)+\sigma(U)\leq J^{\hat{R}-\tilde{c}}(\hat{\pi})+\sigma(U).

(119)

In the last step, we used that
\hat{\pi}
is optimal for
\hat{R}
and thus also
\hat{R}-\tilde{c}
.
Combining Equations (
118
) and (
119
), we obtain the result, Equation (
117
).
∎

Lemma D.18
.

For
q\in(0,1)
, define
g_{q}:(-q,1-q)\to\mathbb{R}
by

g_{q}(x)\coloneqq\log\frac{q+x}{1-(q+x)}.

Then for all
\sigma>0
there exists
\delta(q,\sigma)>0
such that for all
x\in(-q,1-q)
with
|x|<\delta(q,\sigma)
, we have
|g_{q}(x)-g_{q}(0)|<\sigma
.

Concretely, one can choose

\delta(q,\sigma)\coloneqq\big(\exp(\sigma)-1\big)\cdot\min\Bigg\{\frac{1}{\frac{1}{q}+\frac{\exp(\sigma)}{1-q}},\ \ \frac{1}{\frac{1}{1-q}+\frac{\exp(\sigma)}{q}}\Bigg\}

Proof.

If one does not care about the precise quantification,
then the result is simply a reformulation of the continuity of
g_{q}
at the point
x_{0}=0
.

Now we show more specifically that
\delta(q,\sigma)
, as defined above, has the desired property.
Namely, notice the following sequence of equivalences (followed by a one-sided implication) that holds whenever
x\geq 0
:

\displaystyle\big|g_{q}(x)-g_{q}(0)\big|<\sigma\quad

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\quad\log\frac{(q+x)\cdot(1-q)}{\big(1-(q+x)\big)\cdot q}<\sigma

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\quad\frac{(q+x)\cdot(1-q)}{\big(1-(q+x)\big)\cdot q}<\exp(\sigma)

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\quad(q+x)<(1-q-x)\cdot\frac{q}{1-q}\cdot\exp(\sigma)

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\quad\bigg(1+\frac{q}{1-q}\cdot\exp(\sigma)\bigg)\cdot x<q\cdot\big(\exp(\sigma)-1\big)

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\quad x<\frac{\exp(\sigma)-1}{\frac{1}{q}+\frac{\exp(\sigma)}{1-q}}

\displaystyle\Longleftarrow\quad|x|<\delta(q,\sigma).

In the first step, we used the monotonicity of
g_{q}
to get rid of the absolute value.
Similarly, whenever
x\leq 0
, we have

\displaystyle\big|g_{q}(x)-g_{q}(0)\big|<\sigma\quad

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\quad x>\frac{1-\exp(\sigma)}{\frac{1}{1-q}+\frac{\exp(\sigma)}{q}}

\displaystyle\Longleftarrow\quad|x|<\delta(q,\sigma).

This shows the result.
∎

Lemma D.19
.

For
q\in(0,1)
, define
f_{q}:(0,1)\to\mathbb{R}
by

f_{q}(p)\coloneqq-\big[q\log p+(1-q)\log(1-p)\big].

Then for all
\delta>0
there exists
\mu(\delta)>0
such that for all
p\in(0,1)
with
f_{q}(p)<H(q)+\mu(\delta)
, we have
|p-q|<\delta
.
Concretely, one can choose
\mu(\delta)\coloneqq 2\delta^{2}
.

Proof.

Let
\delta>0
and define
\mu(\delta)\coloneqq 2\delta^{2}
.
Assume that
f_{q}(p)<H(q)+\mu(\delta)
.
By Pinker’s inequality, we have

\displaystyle 2(p-q)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq q\log\frac{q}{p}+(1-q)\cdot\log\frac{1-q}{1-p}

\displaystyle=-H(q)+f_{q}(p)

\displaystyle<\mu(\delta)

\displaystyle=2\delta^{2}.

Consequently, we have
|p-q|<\delta
.
∎

Lemma D.20
.

Define
f_{q}(p)
as in Lemma
D.19
.
Then for all
\mu>0
there exists
\epsilon(\mu)>0
such that for all
\hat{R}
with
d^{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(R,\hat{R})<\epsilon(\mu)
, we have the following for all
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\Xi
:

f_{P_{R}(1\mid\xi_{1},\xi_{2})}\big(P_{\hat{R}}(1\mid\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\big)<H\big(P_{R}(1\mid\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\big)+\mu.

Concretely, we can set
\epsilon(\mu)\coloneqq\mu\cdot\min_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\Xi}D(\xi_{1})\cdot D(\xi_{2})

Proof.

We have the following for all
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\Xi
:

\displaystyle\mu\cdot\min_{\xi,\xi^{\prime}}D(\xi)\cdot D(\xi)

\displaystyle=\epsilon(\mu)

\displaystyle>d^{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(R,\hat{R})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\xi,\xi^{\prime}\sim D\times D}\left[{D_{\mathrm{KL}}\Big(P_{R}\big(\cdot\mid\xi,\xi^{\prime}\big)\ \big\|\ P_{\hat{R}}\big(\cdot\mid\xi,\xi^{\prime}\big)\Big)}\right]

\displaystyle\geq\Big(\min_{\xi,\xi^{\prime}}D(\xi)\cdot D(\xi^{\prime})\Big)\cdot D_{\mathrm{KL}}\Big(P_{R}\big(\cdot\mid\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\big)\ \big\|\ P_{\hat{R}}\big(\cdot\mid\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\big)\Big)

Now, Equation (
116
) shows that

D_{\mathrm{KL}}\Big(P_{R}\big(\cdot\mid\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\big)\ \big\|\ P_{\hat{R}}\big(\cdot\mid\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\big)\Big)=f_{P_{R}(1\mid\xi_{1},\xi_{2})}\big(P_{\hat{R}}(1\mid\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\big)-H\big(P_{R}(1\mid\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\big).

The result follows.
∎

Corollary D.21
.

Let
\sigma>0
.
Then there exists
\epsilon\coloneqq\epsilon(\sigma)>0
such that
d^{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(R,\hat{R})<\epsilon
implies that there exists
c\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\big\|G-\big(\hat{G}-c\big)\big\|_{\infty}<\sigma
.

Proof.

Set

\delta\coloneqq\min_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\Xi\times\Xi}\delta\Big(P_{R}(1\mid\xi_{1},\xi_{2}),\sigma\Big),\ \mu\coloneqq\mu(\delta),\ \epsilon\coloneqq\epsilon(\mu),

with the constants satisfying the properties from Lemmas
D.18
,
D.19
, and
D.20
.
Now, let
\hat{R}
be such that
d^{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(R,\hat{R})<\epsilon
.

First of all, Lemma
D.20
ensures that

f_{P_{R}(1\mid\xi_{1},\xi_{2})}\big(P_{\hat{R}}(1\mid\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\big)<H\big(P_{R}(1\mid\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\big)+\mu

for all
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\Xi
.
Then Lemma
D.19
shows that

\big|P_{\hat{R}}(1\mid\xi_{1},\xi_{2})-P_{R}(1\mid\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\big|<\delta

for all
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\Xi
.
From Lemma
D.18
, we obtain that

\bigg|g_{P_{R}(1\mid\xi_{1},\xi_{2})}\Big(P_{\hat{R}}\big(1\mid\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\big)-P_{R}\big(1\mid\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\big)\Big)-g_{P_{R}(1\mid\xi_{1},\xi_{2})}(0)\bigg|<\sigma

(120)

for all
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\Xi
.
Now, note that

g_{P_{R}(1\mid\xi_{1},\xi_{2})}\Big(P_{\hat{R}}\big(1\mid\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\big)-P_{R}\big(1\mid\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\big)\Big)=g_{P_{\hat{R}}(1\mid\xi_{1},\xi_{2})}(0).

Furthermore, for
R^{\prime}\in\{R,\hat{R}\}
, Equation (
115
) leads to the following computation:

\displaystyle g_{P_{R^{\prime}}(1\mid\xi_{1},\xi_{2})}(0)

\displaystyle=\log\frac{P_{R^{\prime}}(1\mid\xi_{1},\xi_{2})}{P_{R^{\prime}}(2\mid\xi_{1},\xi_{2})}

\displaystyle=\log\frac{\exp\big(G^{\prime}(\xi_{1})\big)}{\exp\big(G^{\prime}(\xi_{2})\big)}

\displaystyle=G^{\prime}(\xi_{1})-G^{\prime}(\xi_{2}).

Therefore, Equation (
120
) results in

\Big|\big(\hat{G}(\xi_{1})-G(\xi_{1})\big)-\big(\hat{G}(\xi_{2})-G(\xi_{2})\big)\Big|=\Big|\big(\hat{G}(\xi_{1})-\hat{G}(\xi_{2})\big)-\big(G(\xi_{1})-G(\xi_{2})\big)\Big|<\sigma

for all
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\Xi
.
Now, let
\xi^{*}\in\Xi
be any reference trajectory.
Define
c\coloneqq\hat{G}(\xi^{*})-G(\xi^{*})
.
Then the preceding equation shows that

\big|\hat{G}(\xi)-G(\xi)-c\big|<\sigma

for all
\xi\in\Xi
.
That shows the claim.
∎

Proof of Proposition
D.15
.

We prove Proposition
D.15
by chaining together the constants from the preceding results.
We have
U\in(0,1]
given.
Then, set
\sigma\coloneqq\sigma(U)
and
\epsilon\coloneqq\epsilon(\sigma)
as in Lemma
D.17
and Corollary
D.21
.
Now, let
\hat{R}
be such that
d^{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(R,\hat{R})<\epsilon
and let
\hat{\pi}\in\Pi^{*}(\hat{R})
.
Our goal is to show that
\mathrm{Reg}^{R}\left(\hat{\pi}\right)<U
.

By Corollary
D.21
, there is
c>0
such that
\max_{\xi\in\Xi}\big|\hat{G}(\xi)-G(\xi)-c\big|<\sigma
.
Consequently, Lemma
D.17
ensures that
\mathrm{Reg}^{R}\left(\hat{\pi}\right)<U
.
This was to show.
∎

D.4
Positive result for regularized RLHF

Here, we present simple positive results for regularized RLHF, both in a version with the expected reward distance, and in a version using the distance in choice probabilities.
Some of it will directly draw from the positive results proved before.

Theorem D.22
.

Let
\lambda\in(0,\infty)
be given and fixed.
Assume we are given an MDP
\langle\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},\tau,\mu_{0},R,\gamma\rangle
, and a data distribution
D\in{\mathcal{S}{\times}\mathcal{A}}
which assigns positive probability to all transitions, i.e.,
\forall(s,a)\in{\mathcal{S}{\times}\mathcal{A}},\ D(s,a)>0
.
Let
\omega:\Pi\to\mathbb{R}
be a continuous regularization function that has a reference policy
\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}
as one of its minima.
6
6

6

E.g., if
\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}(a\mid s)>0
for all
(s,a)\in{\mathcal{S}{\times}\mathcal{A}}
and
\omega(\pi)\coloneqq\mathbb{D_{\text{KL}}}\left({\pi}||{\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}}\right)
, then the minimum is given by
\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}
.

Assume that
\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}
is
not

(\lambda,\omega)
-optimal for
R
and let
L=\mathrm{Reg}^{R}\left(\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}\right)
. Then there exists
\epsilon>0
such that D
\in\mathrm{\mathbf{safe}}(R,\epsilon,L,\lambda,\omega)
.

Proof.

We prove the theorem by showing that for every
D\in\Delta({\mathcal{S}{\times}\mathcal{A}})
such that
D(s,a)>0
for all
(s,a)\in{\mathcal{S}{\times}\mathcal{A}}
, there exists
\epsilon>0
such that for all
\hat{R}
with
\mathbb{E}_{(s,a)\sim D}\left[{\frac{|\hat{R}(s,a)-R(s,a)|}{\mathrm{range}\ R}}\right]<\epsilon
and all policies
\hat{\pi}
that are
(\lambda,\omega)
-RLHF optimal wrt.
\hat{R}
, we have
\mathrm{Reg}^{R}\left(\hat{\pi}\right)<\mathrm{Reg}^{R}\left(\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}\right)
. Because
L=\mathrm{Reg}^{R}\left(\hat{\pi}\right)<\mathrm{Reg}^{R}\left(\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}\right)
this proves that then
D\in\mathrm{\mathbf{safe}}(R,\epsilon,L,\lambda,\omega)
.

The proof is an application of Berge’s maximum Theorem, Theorem
D.3
.
Namely, define the function

f:\mathcal{R}\times\Pi\to\mathbb{R},\quad f(R,\pi)\coloneqq J_{R}(\pi)-\lambda\omega(\pi).

Furthermore, define the correspondence
C:\mathcal{R}\rightrightarrows\Pi
as the trivial map
C(R)=\Pi
.
Let
f^{*}:\mathcal{R}\to\mathbb{R}
map a reward function to the value of a
(\lambda,\omega)
-RLHF optimal policy, i.e.,
f^{*}(R)\coloneqq\max_{\pi\in\Pi}f(R,\pi)
.
Define
C^{*}
as the corresponding argmax, i.e.,
C^{*}(R)\coloneqq\big\{\pi\mid f(R,\pi)=f^{*}(R)\big\}
.
Assume on
\mathcal{R}
we have the standard Euclidean topology.
Since
\omega
is assumed continuous and by Proposition
D.4
also
J
is continuous, it follows that
f
is continuous.
Thus, Theorem
D.3
implies that
C^{*}
is upper hemicontinuous, see Definition
D.2
.
The rest of the proof is simply an elaboration of why upper hemicontinuity of
C^{*}
gives the result.

Now, define the set

\mathcal{V}\coloneqq\big\{\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi\mid\mathrm{Reg}^{R}\left(\pi^{\prime}\right)<\mathrm{Reg}^{R}\left(\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}\right)\big\}.

Since the regret is a continuous function, this set is open.
Now, let
\pi\in C^{*}(R)
be
(\lambda,\omega)
-RLHF optimal with respect to
R
.
It follows

\displaystyle J_{R}(\pi)

\displaystyle=f(R,\pi)+\lambda\omega(\pi)

\displaystyle>f(R,\pi_{\mathrm{ref}})+\lambda\omega(\pi_{\mathrm{ref}})

\displaystyle=J_{R}(\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}),

where we used the optimality of
\pi
for
f
, that
\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}
is not optimal for it, and that
\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}
is the minimum of
\omega
.
So overall, this shows
C^{*}(R)\subseteq\mathcal{V}
.

Since
C^{*}
is upper hemicontinuous, this means there exists an open set
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathcal{R}
with
R\in\mathcal{U}
and such that for all
\hat{R}\in\mathcal{U}
, we have
C^{*}(\hat{R})\subseteq\mathcal{V}
.
Let
\epsilon>0
be so small that all reward functions
\hat{R}
with
\mathbb{E}_{(s,a)\sim D}\left[{\frac{|\hat{R}(s,a)-R(s,a)|}{\mathrm{range}\ R}}\right]<\epsilon
satisfy
\hat{R}\in\mathcal{U}
— which exists since
\mathcal{U}
is open in the Euclidean topology.
Then for all such
\hat{R}
and any policy
\hat{\pi}
that is
(\lambda,\omega)
-RLHF optimal wrt.
\hat{R}
, we by definition have

\hat{\pi}\in C^{*}(\hat{R})\subseteq\mathcal{V},

and thus, by definition of
\mathcal{V}
, the desired regret property.
This was to show.
∎

Now, we show the same result, but with the choice distance instead of expected reward distance:

Theorem D.23
.

Let
\lambda\in(0,\infty)
be given and fixed.
Assume we are given an MDP
\langle\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},\tau,\mu_{0},R,\gamma\rangle
, and a data distribution
D\in{\mathcal{S}{\times}\mathcal{A}}
which assigns positive probability to all transitions, i.e.,
\forall(s,a)\in{\mathcal{S}{\times}\mathcal{A}},\ D(s,a)>0
.
Let
\omega:\Pi\to\mathbb{R}
be a continuous regularization function that has a reference policy
\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}
as one of its minima.
Assume that
\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}
is
not

(\lambda,\omega)
-optimal for
R
and let
L=\mathrm{Reg}^{R}\left(\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}\right)
. Then there exists
\epsilon>0
such that
D\in\mathrm{\mathbf{safe}}^{\mathbb{D_{\text{KL}}}}\big(R,\epsilon,L,\lambda,\omega\big)
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{G}\coloneqq\mathbb{R}^{\Xi}
be the vector space of return functions, which becomes a topological space when equipped with the infinity norm.
Define the function

\displaystyle f:\mathcal{G}\times\Pi\to\mathbb{R},\quad f(G,\pi)\coloneqq J^{G}(\pi)-\lambda\omega(\pi),

where
J^{G}(\pi)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}_{\xi\sim\pi}\left[{G(\xi)}\right]
is the policy evaluation function of the return function
G
.

f
is continuous.
Define the correspondence
C:\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows\Pi
as the trivial map
C(G)=\Pi
.
Let
f^{*}:\mathcal{G}\to\mathbb{R}
map a return function to the value of a
(\lambda,\omega)
-optimal policy, i.e.,
f^{*}(G)\coloneqq\max_{\pi\in\Pi}f(G,\pi)
.
Define
C^{*}
as the corresponding argmax.
Then Theorem
D.3
implies that
C^{*}
is upper hemicontinuous, see Definition
D.2
.
As in the previous proof, the rest is an elaboration of why this gives the desired result.

Set
G
as the return function corresponding to
R
.
Define

\mathcal{V}\coloneqq\big\{\pi^{\prime}\in\Pi\ |\ \mathrm{Reg}^{R}\left(\pi^{\prime}\right)<L\big\}.

We now claim that
C^{*}(G)\subseteq\mathcal{V}
.
Indeed, let
\pi\in C^{*}(G)
.
Then

\displaystyle J^{R}(\pi)

\displaystyle=f(G,\pi)+\lambda\omega(\pi)

\displaystyle>f(G,\pi_{\mathrm{ref}})+\lambda\omega(\pi_{\mathrm{ref}})

\displaystyle=J^{R}(\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}).

Note that we used the optimality of
\pi
for
f
, that
\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}
is not optimal for it, and also that
\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}
minimizes
\omega
by assumption.
This shows
\mathrm{Reg}^{R}\left(\pi\right)<\mathrm{Reg}^{R}\left(\pi_{\mathrm{ref}}\right)=L
, and thus the claim.

Since
C^{*}
is upper hemicontinuous and
\mathcal{V}
an open set, this implies that there exists
\sigma>0
such that for all
\hat{G}\in\mathcal{G}
with
\big\|G-\hat{G}\big\|_{\infty}<\sigma
, we have
C^{*}(\hat{G})\subseteq\mathcal{V}
.

Now, define
\epsilon\coloneqq\epsilon(\sigma)
as in Corollary
D.21
and let
\hat{R}
be any reward function with
d^{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(R,\hat{R})<\epsilon
.
Then by that corollary, there exists
c\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\big\|G-\big(\hat{G}-c\big)\big\|_{\infty}<\sigma
.
Consequently, we have
C^{*}(\hat{G})=C^{*}(\hat{G}-c)\subseteq\mathcal{V}
by what we showed before, which shows the result.
∎
